SpaceX now fundamentally an "infrastructure company," senior official says
2026-11-09

SpaceX now fundamentally an "infrastructure company," senior official says

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
For a long time, SpaceX has been synonymous with the development of newer, larger, and ever-more-reusable rockets. But that began to change over the last decade with the introduction of Starlink, and the change has accelerated during the last 18 months as the company has pivoted to data and artificial intelligence. At last week’s UP.Summit conference in Florida, the vice president of launch at SpaceX, Kiko Dontchev, offered some of the most revealing comments to date about the company’s changing focus, how it is embracing AI, and why the Falcon 9 rocket—which has dominated global launch for a decade and remains the world’s preeminent launch vehicle—is firmly on the road to retirement. “In the last couple years, when I talk about SpaceX, yes, we are a rocket company, but fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point,” Dontchev said. “We’re building infrastructure on the ground and low Earth orbit, soon on the Moon, and obviously eventually Mars.” Earlier this year, SpaceX acquired one of founder Elon Musk’s other companies, xAI. SpaceX engineers were already using xAI’s tools, Dontchev said, but in recent months, employees have really started to figure out how artificial intelligence can speed up their work. “What’s happened at SpaceX is we’ve really gone through a huge acceleration of adoption,” Dontchev said. “Even just a year ago, we weren’t adopting it that quickly, but really starting this year, we started to adopt it extremely rapidly. And the acceleration we have seen in our ability to do engineering is unlike anything I’ve ever seen at the company in my 16 years.” Tasks that would take SpaceX employees hours to days now take seconds to minutes, he said. And it’s not just the engineers. Dontchev said the work of identifying foreign object debris, or FOD, in aerospace parts has been largely automated using the AI assistant Grok. This debris can cause catastrophic damage to engines and other spacecraft components. “We’ve automated being able to detect FOD,” he said. “We’ll have an inspector go in and borescope a tube, and we find that humans are about 94 percent perfect (at) finding a piece of it. Grok analyzing the same video is 99.9 percent perfect.” SpaceX has also automated visual inspections of other hardware it produces, such as Starlink customer terminals, due to similar gains in accuracy. Dontchev said AI is now being used at every level of the company’s operations. “We’ve truly started to integrate it to where every single person, including the technicians and the technician leaders, are starting to use it in their day-to-day, and it’s completely leveled the playing field and accelerated people’s growth,” he said. Since SpaceX’s founding in 2002, a community of spaceflight enthusiasts gravitated to the company because of Musk’s vision for humanity becoming a multiplanetary species, beginning with the settlement of Mars. But concern has grown in this group that SpaceX is drifting away from that vision. Musk has recently made a hard pivot toward the deployment of orbital data centers, envisioning hundreds of thousands of solar-powered satellites providing computing services to users on Earth. Musk has largely stopped talking about sending humans to Mars, except in passing. Dontchev insisted that human settlement and Mars remain part of the core of SpaceX’s aspirations. “We have come to understand that this tool is critical to us accelerating our pace of innovation,” he said of artificial intelligence. “Without AI, I think we still would have gotten to the Moon. I think we still get to Mars with SI. We’re going to get there so much faster.” Dontchev arrived at SpaceX just before the company flew its first Falcon 9 rocket in mid-2010. But because the company was never able to solve second-stage reuse, SpaceX needs to move on, he said. “Falcon 9, I love that rocket to heart,” he said. “I’ve launched many of them, all of them, in fact. But it’s still not fully reusable. We still throw that second stage away every single flight. Starship—we’re going to catch both the first stage and the second stage.” For 100 launches a year, it is possible to fly a partially reusable rocket, he said. But for a company that aspires to launch thousands of rockets a year, and eventually maybe even 10,000, the cost and time lost in manufacturing a new second stage for every launch is simply not sustainable. It is also significant that Dontchev spoke less about Starship’s payload capacity and more about its productivity per launch. The first orbital Starship launch, in late September, carried larger Starlink V3 satellites. And in the aggregate, this single mission carried 10 times the bandwidth of the Starlink payload launched by a Falcon 9. Flying Starship from Florida, he said, will further improve capacity compared to Texas launches. Launching from Florida offers more favorable trajectories to certain orbits, potentially allowing Starship to carry more satellites without expending propellant on dogleg maneuvers. Dontchev said a single Florida Starship launch will be able to carry up to 20 times the bandwidth of a single Falcon 9. To that end, the pad infrastructure for Starship’s first launch tower at Kennedy Space Center’s Launch Complex 39A is nearly finished. The company is also developing two additional pads in Florida. “Pad 39A is effectively complete,” he said. “We’re going to transport boosters and ships over barge here to the Cape. We’re hoping to actually do the first transports this year in December, and then maybe even fly by the end of the year.” Starship is clearly being optimized to serve SpaceX’s launch needs. But what about commercial launch customers that have relied on Falcon 9 rockets over the last decade? During his remarks, Dontchev did not address widespread concerns in the US commercial space industry and beyond that SpaceX has not moved to replace the Falcon 9’s lost capacity with Starship. For now, multiple sources have indicated, SpaceX is holding Starship launches in the coming years for its own needs, and potentially those of NASA and other government customers.

Nvidia CEO's affair becomes a national security problem
2026-11-09

Nvidia CEO's affair becomes a national security problem

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Nvidia’s chief executive Jensen Huang has admitted having an affair two days after the FT reported his alleged romantic relationship with a woman the US intelligence community believes was working as a spy for China. Huang revealed the affair in an internal message to employees on Sunday that was obtained by the FT. “I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang said in the message. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.” In a separate statement, Nvidia said its board would open an independent investigation “in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.” Huang said he would fully cooperate. “My responsibility is to serve you and be worthy of your trust,” he said. “I have fallen short of that responsibility. I am fully committed to Nvidia and will work to earn back your confidence.” The FT reported on Friday that the US believed Huang had a romantic relationship with a Chinese woman who was recruited to spy for China’s Ministry of State Security intelligence agency. American officials this year wrote a memo, based on information from US intelligence, which said the Nvidia boss had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based customer of the chipmaker, according to people familiar with the document. The affair is a damaging blow to the Nvidia chief, who in recent years has become the face of the AI boom, one of the technology’s loudest champions, and transformed the Silicon Valley group into the first $5 trillion company. It also comes at a critical juncture for the wider AI sector, which faces a deepening political backlash in the US and growing investor concern over whether the hundreds of billions of dollars spent on infrastructure will pay off. Nvidia’s Frankfurt-listed shares were down more than 6 percent in early trading on Monday. Huang’s admission of an affair comes after Nvidia previously dismissed suggestions of an improper relationship with Alice Huang, saying the interactions were “nothing unusual or unexpected” when dealing with an Nvidia customer. The American government memo, which said US intelligence believed the Megaspeed executive had been recruited by the MSS, was drafted just before President Donald Trump traveled to Beijing for a summit with Xi Jinping. Jensen Huang has developed a close relationship with Trump, which has frustrated US national security officials who believe he is too willing to sell advanced chips to China that could harm American security. Asked about Huang on Sunday, Trump told reporters he had done an “amazing job” for the US. Asked if he regretted not acting on the intelligence about the affair, Trump said: “No, I don’t think so. I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it.” “I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what is going on,” Trump added. After broadly staying out of politics, Huang started to build relations with Trump after the president called him out of the blue on his birthday. He quickly became one of Trump’s favorite executives, accompanying him on state visits, including to China earlier this year and the UK last year. Huang has repeatedly lobbied Trump to adopt a more permissive approach to shipping Nvidia’s AI chips to China, after the company was entirely cut off from the critical market by new export controls in 2025. The resulting deal—with Trump permitting the export of Nvidia’s older-generation H200 chips to China, with the US government taking a cut of sales—had a minimal impact on the Silicon Valley company’s sales, as Beijing pushed back against its technology. But it further angered China hawks in the US administration. The US government had already been investigating Megaspeed over concerns that it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China. Nvidia on Sunday said a committee of independent directors, helped by external lawyers, would oversee the investigation. “The board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders,” the company said. “While the board has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, the board’s inquiry will examine all available information.” Huang, who co-founded Nvidia as a video-game graphics card company in 1993, is one of the longest-serving founder-CEOs in Silicon Valley. More than most technology groups, Nvidia’s success is tethered to that of its CEO. Huang leads a close and loyal core team of about half a dozen executives, acting as its chief salesman on the international stage while directly managing dozens of direct reports who keep the company running. He has credited this “flat” organizational structure for the speed at which the company operates, helping it to stay ahead of rivals in the AI chip market and increasing its market valuation from roughly $1 trillion in 2023 to $4.5 trillion today. Elon Musk, a close ally of Huang, was among the first to offer support, agreeing with a social media post that he was “one of the greatest entrepreneurs of this generation” and “irreplaceable for America’s AI leadership.” But John Moolenaar, the Republican head of the House China committee who, with other lawmakers, has requested Huang to testify before Congress, said: “It is vital that Jensen Huang address our national security concerns in Congress related to China’s access to sensitive technology and information.” © 2026 The Financial Times Ltd. All rights reserved. Not to be redistributed, copied, or modified in any way.

F1 in Brazil: You’ve got to keep your tires alive
2026-11-09

F1 in Brazil: You’ve got to keep your tires alive

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
In sports, a “dead rubber” is a match that takes place after the outcome of the series or championship has already been decided. The people in charge hate this*—if you wrap up a title two-thirds of the way through a season, the viewers evaporate, as there’s nothing left at stake. But anyone who decided to skip this year’s Brazilian Grand Prix this past weekend missed one of the year’s most entertaining races, as a combination of a mixed-up starting order, some very fast drivers at the back, and a wet track that dried and then saw rain again resulted in action aplenty. A peculiarity of F1 is that even if you manage to win a championship, there’s no guarantee you’ll be in a position to defend it. Take McLaren’s Lando Norris, who took the crown in 2025 after a nail-biting fight with his teammate Oscar Piastri and Red Bull’s Max Verstappen. Sadly for Lando, McLaren did not start this year in a position to win races, and even with several upgrades, the team has produced a car that is still very tricky to drive. But this weekend in Brazil, Norris demonstrated the form that earned him the number 1. Rain on Saturday during qualifying helped jumble the usual running order. It was heavy enough that drivers used the wet weather tire with deep treads, which usually generates so much spray that visibility disappears and running has to be suspended. But with fewer cars on track in the later knockout qualifying sessions, this was not a problem for once. More disruption came from grid penalties for Verstappen and Ferrari’s Lewis Hamilton, both of whom needed to use extra power unit components outside of the season’s allowance. Norris claimed pole, with this year’s new champion, Kimi Antonelli, alongside in his Mercedes. Row two was filled with the unusual sight of Liam Lawson’s Racing Bull and Nico Hulkenberg’s Audi, followed by Red Bull’s Isack Hadjar and Oliver Bearman’s Haas. Next was George Russell in the other Mercedes, then the even more unusual sight of Fernando Alonso’s Aston Martin, normally found much farther back. One could say the same about William’s Alex Albon, who qualified ninth ahead of the second McLaren of Oscar Piastri. It looked like it was going wrong for Norris right from the start of the race. Antonelli made a better start and passed him into turn 1. Lawson followed through into second place at the next corner. But Norris kept his head down, and by lap six, he was past the Racing Bull, with about five seconds to make up on Antonelli. Well-timed pit stops saw Norris switch to the right tires at the right time, and he made none of his rivals’ mistakes, even when the rain returned in the final few laps—at least until the podium ceremony, when he tried to fizz his champagne bottle but instead shattered it. Keeping one’s tires in the right operating window was vitally important in Brazil, but more than a few struggled with it. The Mercedes duo appeared to struggle to generate sufficient tire temperatures, and their cars looked difficult to handle, prompting plenty of complaints from both Russell and Antonelli to the garage. Russell had been progressing well and ran in the top three early in the race but spun after leaving the pit lane for his first stop, ending up facing the wrong way. A few laps later, Antonelli spun his own car from the lead, this time going into turn 1, letting Norris back into first. Antonelli then did it again four laps later, ending fourth. Eventually, the Mercedes finished with Russell in fourth and Antonelli in fifth. But the team sealed its Constructors’ Championship, so there was still plenty of celebrating despite the relatively mediocre results (by Mercedes’ high standards). It’s worth watching the in-car footage from the first couple of laps from both Hamilton and Verstappen, who lined up 20th and 21st out of 22 cars. By the end of lap 1, Hamilton was already in ninth and making things look easy—then again, Brazil was his 400th F1 race start, and he has won more than a quarter of them. But within four laps, his charge had been blunted. Getting his rear tires up to temperature was key to making up so much ground, but with the track drying, the intermediate tires everyone started the race on had a finite life. With his used up, Hamilton started sliding back down the order. On two occasions, he ended up racing his teammate Charles Leclerc, who started mid-pack with his own penalty. Unlike in Mexico, Italy, or Holland, the two red cars were able to play nice with each other. But neither managed their tires well, and the best they could do on the road was seventh (Leclerc) and ninth (Hamilton), which became eighth for Leclerc after he was given a five-second penalty for rejoining the track unsafely after running off track while battling Lawson. Verstappen’s first lap wasn’t quite as good as Hamilton’s—the Red Bull was also never entirely within its setup window all weekend. But taking longer to turn on the rear tires probably contributed to their longevity. While Hamilton’s charge through the pack was blunted, Verstappen was in ninth place by lap 4. By lap 24, he was in second, having taken advantage of Antonelli’s second spin. Verstappen would eventually finish the day in second, 14 seconds off Norris. F1 takes a weekend off next week, then returns for the night race in Las Vegas. *Perhaps the prime example of this is NASCAR, which switched to a fairly incomprehensible playoff series it has continued to tweak for years in an effort to prevent anyone from winning the big cup until the last race of the year.
Ars Technica - All content
New Democratic Congress will likely strip the gilding from Trump's Golden Dome
2026-11-09

New Democratic Congress will likely strip the gilding from Trump's Golden Dome

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Two of the top lawmakers backing the Pentagon’s Golden Dome missile defense shield are among the wave of Republicans leaving Congress in the coming Democratic takeover, raising questions about the future of President Donald Trump’s signature second-term defense initiative. The shakeup on Capitol Hill comes after military officials signaled in recent months that they might backpedal from one element Trump asked for in Golden Dome: the capability to shoot down missiles in their initial boost phase shortly after launch. The Golden Dome has escaped serious congressional scrutiny since Trump announced the program in January 2025, one week into his second term in the White House. That lack of scrutiny will change as Democrats take responsibility for writing the Pentagon’s budget, introduce limits on funding, and put forth bills to strip out Golden Dome’s most controversial component and give the program a new name. That’s the opinion of a half-dozen current and former officials in industry and government. Ars spoke with sources before and after last week’s congressional elections to get a sense of how a new Democratic Congress might make its imprint on missile defense. It’s important to know that much of what the Trump administration calls “Golden Dome” was already in development (or in some cases, operational) when Trump signed the executive order outlining the initiative 22 months ago. Trump’s order created a new program to consolidate the Pentagon’s existing missile defense efforts, accelerate work already underway on new Ground-Based Interceptors (GBIs), and deploy a new layer of orbiting sensors and Space-Based Interceptors (SBIs) to defend the US homeland against both traditional ballistic missiles and new emerging threats like drones and hypersonic weapons. The Golden Dome was immediately politicized. The first tranche of its funding—approximately $24 billion—was part of Trump’s so-called “One Big Beautiful Bill,” along with some of the White House’s other contentious policy priorities. The bill passed Congress on a party-line vote. This meant the budget for Golden Dome was not part of the normal military budgeting process in Congress, which usually involves both parties. Instead, it passed Congress through a controversial shortcut known as reconciliation. Sen. Mike Rounds (R-S.D.) said in a September hearing that the White House’s decision to link Golden Dome’s first tranche of funding to a partisan reconciliation bill was unnecessary. “This should have been on a bipartisan basis,” Rounds said. Indeed, Capitol Hill Republicans refused the Trump administration’s effort to add $350 billion to the Pentagon’s budget this year through a similar reconciliation mechanism. Lawmakers also formed an all-Republican “Golden Dome Caucus” soon after the unveiling of Golden Dome. Seven House members of the caucus either lost their reelection bids or did not run for reelection at all in the 2026 midterms. These include the caucus’s co-chair, Rep. Jeff Crank (R-Colo.), and the chair of the House subcommittee overseeing Golden Dome, Rep. Scott DesJarlais (R-Tenn.). Both are among the Golden Dome’s biggest cheerleaders in Congress. The Democratic ranking member of the House Subcommittee on Strategic Forces, Rep. Scott Moulton (D-Mass.), is retiring from Congress. That leaves Rep. John Garamendi (D-Calif.) as the next-senior Democrat on the subcommittee, with a path to becoming chairman when the new Congress convenes in January. In remarks earlier this year, he called Golden Dome a “massive, extraordinarily expensive, and ultimately inadequate defensive system.” “When you use the words Golden Dome, now it’s political, and when you politicize national security, you get a political response, so it is damaging,” said John Plumb, who supervised military space policy for the Biden administration, in a panel discussion at the CyberSat conference this week. The Trump administration projects Golden Dome will cost $185 billion over the next 10 years, a figure that’s hard to verify with the scarce information military officials have released about how the system will work. Other cost estimates are significantly higher. The Congressional Budget Office concluded that a national missile defense system meeting the requirements set out in Trump’s executive order would cost about $1.2 trillion. The American Enterprise Institute released a report last year with a menu of potential Golden Dome architectures, ranging from a quarter-trillion dollars (without SBIs) to $3.6 trillion at the high end. Pentagon officials chafe at these numbers, claiming they do not take into account the military’s assumptions for low-cost launches and mass-produced satellite networks. The military envisions at least hundreds, and perhaps thousands, of Space-Based Interceptors in the uppermost layer of Golden Dome. These interceptors would loiter in orbit to wait for a missile attack. SBIs have never been placed into orbit before, despite decades of research and development. The idea has been around at least since the 1980s, when former President Ronald Reagan pushed the Strategic Defense Initiative, or “Star Wars,” weapons program. “The most ambitious element of Golden Dome is the Space-Based Interceptor layer, and that is going to face significant technical challenges,” said Sen. Mark Kelly (D-Ariz.), in a congressional hearing earlier this year. “The cost, the physics, the likely reliability, in my view, do not support a full-scale space-based intercept architecture,” said Kelly, a former Navy combat pilot and astronaut. “This means that we could spend more than a trillion dollars and end up with a system that’s not going to work, and it’s not going to meaningfully improve our security.” Some critics call out Golden Dome’s potential negative impact on global stability, fearing it could spark an arms race driving the world’s other nuclear powers to expand their arsenals. “It motivates the other side to just build more missiles because the easiest way to overcome a defense like that is just to buy more,” said Frank Kendall, who served as secretary of the Air Force in the Biden administration. Others point to the risks of weaponizing space—a similar argument used against the Reagan-era Star Wars program. Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink lifted that taboo in September with the first public disclosure that the US Space Force already has weapons in orbit. But there’s more to the story than SBIs. The satellites and radars that detect and track missile launches are either in use today or were already in development during previous administrations. The same goes for Ground-Based Interceptors, which have been deployed for decades to take out missiles as they approach their targets. Under President Biden, the Pentagon’s Missile Defense Agency awarded Lockheed Martin a $17 billion contract to develop a next-generation Ground-Based Interceptor to defend against more sophisticated missile threats. The $24 billion Congress approved for Golden Dome last year was a one-time allocation for fiscal year 2026, which ended on October 1. The program’s funding dried up last month because the government is currently operating under a continuing resolution—an extension of last year’s regular military budget, which, of course, did not include the $24 billion Golden Dome infusion routed through reconciliation. The preliminary funding bills in the House and Senate for fiscal year 2027, if passed as-is, would provide only a fraction of the more than $17 billion the Trump administration requested for Golden Dome in this budget cycle. That’s because the Trump administration again separated a significant part of its record $1.5 trillion topline military budget request into a reconciliation package outside of the normal congressional appropriations process. The supplemental $350 billion funding request—which included funds for Golden Dome—went nowhere in the Republican-controlled Congress. And it won’t happen when Democrats take the gavels next year, either. It’s impossible to judge the realism of the Pentagon’s aim to get a first draft of Golden Dome up and running by the end 0f 2028, before the end of Trump’s presidency, because so much of the system is classified—but skepticism is the safer bet. Military officials have said little about Golden Dome’s design, architecture, or concept of operations, citing a national security imperative for secrecy. One thing we do know is that a homeland defense shield like Golden Dome will need international partners to host radar sensors, ground stations, and missile batteries. Canada is an obvious target for such a partnership, and a fully deployed Golden Dome would notionally provide protection over all of North America. However, this part of the program is off to a slow start. Michael Guetlein, the four-star Space Force general in charge of Golden Dome development, said earlier this year that tariffs—a core Trump administration policy—were limiting his interactions with foreign governments. So what can we expect when the next Congress convenes with Democratic majorities in January? The consensus among the experts Ars interviewed is that military leaders will still push for new options in missile defense, a need they say is underscored by their experience during the US war with Iran and Russia’s persistent missile strikes on Ukraine. Modern missiles and drones can easily dodge existing defense systems. The stark expense of existing Western interceptors compared to the cost of Iranian missiles and drones can make defenders think twice about whether to shoot or keep their powder dry for a future attack. “I feel like the entire effort would benefit from a better problem statement,” one former military space official told Ars. “Why do we think we need something that we don’t currently have? What’s changed? What’s different? What is it?” This official offered some answers to these questions: “We are not in the 1970s or 1980s anymore, where it was us and the Soviet Union. The vast share of strategic threats to us were strategic ballistic missiles that we could see coming. That’s how things used to be.” “Today’s environment is different,” he continued. “You have a host of strategic threats that are making classic ballistic missiles shrink in share. You’ve got hypersonic weapons. You’ve got endo-atmospheric hypersonic vehicles. You have increasingly accurate and harder to detect cruise missiles. It’s no longer just Russia. It’s China now, and to a lesser extent, North Korea and Iran.” These new missile threats and the ubiquity of one-way attack drones make US and allied bases more susceptible to attack, the official said. “What do we do about it? Do we just build more Mutually Assured Destruction platforms, or is there something else we can do? I think that’s what gets you to maybe we should look at ways to at least interfere with a potential attack against us.” But some parts of Golden Dome, if followed to the letter of Trump’s executive order, simply may not work, at least at the scale required for global coverage, the official told Ars. One of them is a requirement to develop SBIs that can destroy enemy missiles during their boost phase soon after launch. This is in contrast to midcourse intercept in space or terminal intercept as the missile homes in on its target. “The boost-phase intercept is a much bigger challenge,” the former military official said. “I’m probably going to have to have an interceptor that can survive reentry, track through reentry, and hit something while it’s still in the atmosphere. Hitting something in midcourse, as it’s left the atmosphere and is in space is just an easier problem… The original executive order talked about boost-phase intercept, and I was always skeptical about that one.” An SBI aimed at a boost-phase ballistic missile would have to look down toward the Earth, not up toward space. The interceptor would have to distinguish the missile it’s tracking against the background of Earth, with clouds, cities, land, and water. It’s much easier to do that against the cold blackness of space. There are some benefits to a boost-phase intercept. For example, the ballistic missile is traveling slower soon after launch, and you might have another shot to intercept it if you miss. The window for intercepting a missile in boost phase is also exceptionally narrow. The time from a launch detection to a shoot-down decision might be less than a minute, and a commander charged with making a “kill decision” would likely want more time to understand what’s happening before pulling the trigger on an interceptor. “When does the person who has the authority for weapons engagement release the weapon? If your answer is it’s all automated, that’s interesting, but it’s also a little scary,” the former military official said. “Now I’m going to explain to the president why you just killed three taikonauts (Chinese astronauts) coming off a launch pad because no human was there. If you do need a human to say, ‘OK, I authorize release of the weapon,’ then that’s a time gap.” Guetlein, the man in charge of Golden Dome, has acknowledged this challenge. “I do believe that the technology exists to get after that threat today, and we believe we can solve it, but… can I do it at scale, and can I do it affordably? That’s going to be the huge challenge for boost-phase intercept,” Guetlein told lawmakers in an April hearing. “I will tell you, because we are so focused on affordability, if we cannot do it affordably, we will not go into production.” There are other ways to take down missiles if engineers can’t close the case for boost-phase intercept, Guetlein said. “If boost-phase intercept from space is not affordable and [doesn’t] scale well, we will not produce it because we have other options to get after it.” If proven feasible, directed-energy weapons using microwave or laser beams would provide US forces near-endless “magazine depth” in responding to massive salvos of missiles and drones, eschewing the need for “kinetic” SBIs for missile defense. Sen. Angus King (I-Maine) said in a hearing earlier this year that the Space Force should spend more on directed-energy research than on space-based kinetic kill vehicles. Plumb, the former Biden military space policy chief, pointed to bipartisan support for the Pentagon’s existing missile defense programs, along with new initiatives to improve tracking hypersonic missiles. “I think any administration, Republican or Democrat, is going to continue to improve missile defenses for the US, 100 percent,” he said, adding that it is “probably unlikely” that SBIs survive the coming cull. So what do the people actually building Golden Dome think? Their answer is similar. Some missile defense priorities will survive, but maybe with a new name. The name Golden Dome is a “brand,” said Dylan Taylor, chairman and CEO of Voyager Technologies, which works with Anduril on SBIs. “It’s a proxy for layers of a protective onion, if you will, and there are elements of what is being called Golden Dome, where the technology is very advanced, and it works. The systems work. They’re more functional than I think people have reported on or have indicated.” “Golden Dome is a lot more than SBIs,” said another executive at a space company working on the project. “It’s one layer and element. We believe that there’s bipartisan support for this capability because it is a required capability for national security. The question is whether it can be achieved at a price that makes it executable and palatable. The technical aspect of this closes, and there’s just a question about scalability and cost.” The executive told Ars that newer, smaller space companies can do the job for less money than the Pentagon’s legacy contractors. Many companies are spending private capital on Golden Dome-related research and development with the promise of future Pentagon business. Military leaders have added stops on Wall Street and with venture capital firms to their usual tours of bases, command centers, and test ranges. “There’s real hardware being built by all the players,” the official said, suggesting optimism about fielding a working preliminary version of Golden Dome by 2028. “From us, on our execution to date, we’ve conducted engine tests and thermal tests and communications tests. “My fiduciary responsibility is to turn our investment into these critical technologies into future value, and I wouldn’t have made that call had I not been comfortable with the survivability of the program, assuming we can achieve the technical and financial targets, which we’ve convinced ourselves we can,” the official said. Rocket Lab is another company with business ties to Golden Dome. The company’s founder and CEO, Peter Beck, told Ars he was convinced missile defense will remain a priority no matter who controls Congress or the White House. “Is it a national security imperative that we go to the Moon? Probably not. Is it a national security imperative that we can protect the nation from incoming missile threats? Yes,” Beck said. “With these sorts of things, I’d say it’s what shape and scale they are, not whether they survive.” “There are elements of Golden Dome that I don’t think are going to be politically vulnerable, and there will be other parts of Golden Dome that, of course, will be,” Taylor said. “The point I want to emphasize is people who say, ‘Well, Golden Dome is dead; the whole thing is dead,’ they don’t necessarily understand that there are four or five or six layers to the system, and some are vulnerable and some are not.” Voyager’s expertise for missile defense is in advanced propulsion, and Taylor said those technologies are relevant for a range of systems, not just Golden Dome. “There’s no doubt that whatever it’s going to be called, it won’t be called Golden Dome, and there will definitely be machinations in the system, but it won’t be a ‘throw the baby out with the bath water’ approach,” Taylor said. “Given the risk profile, I don’t see that happening.” In a Senate hearing in September, Tom Karako, director of the Missile Defense Project at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, proposed the idea of a name change as one option to “depoliticize” the missile defense issue. “It is no secret that the ‘Golden Dome’ moniker has contributed to political polarization around the effort,” Karako said in his testimony. “Several options exist to depolarize and depoliticize the effort. It belongs to Congress to name or rename executive departments—witness the pending proposal to rename the Department of Defense as the Department of War. Congress likewise has legislative power to rename the Golden Dome initiative in a future national defense authorization act.” “There are things in the program that make sense,” said Sen. Kelly, who serves on the Senate Subcommittee for Strategic Forces. These include new capabilities for “detecting launches,” he said. “The Space-Based Interceptor part is really, really hard, and the physics clearly favors the offense,” Kelly said. “We could go down this road, spend hundreds of billions of dollars, and not get any return on that investment, and missile defense is too important to pursue this just based on aspiration alone.” Kathy Warden, the CEO of Northrop Grumman, acknowledged last year that with SBIs, “more invention is required than other parts of the [Golden Dome] architecture.” The most recent trends in warfare, as seen in the Middle East and Ukraine, highlight the need for cheaper, mass-produced countermeasures to use against massive drone and missile attacks. In other words, it requires magazine depth, as some military commanders like to say. Democrats seem likely to conclude that the path to magazine depth does not include SBIs.
Ars Technica - All content
Physicists listened to electrical noise to crack a superconductor mystery
2026-11-08

Physicists listened to electrical noise to crack a superconductor mystery

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
Superconductors carry electricity without resistance, but most only work a few degrees above absolute zero. In the late 1980s, copper-based materials called cuprates were found to superconduct at higher temperatures—reachable with affordable liquid nitrogen. Yet nearly 40 years later, physicists still don’t agree on why. In a recent Science study, a team of physicists led by Milan Allan of Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich and Ingmar Swart of Utrecht University in the Netherlands has found some answers by listening to the faint electrical crackle of current flowing into a cuprate. Central to the mystery of cuprates’ high-temperature superconductivity is a strange state called the pseudogap. “The pseudogap is a phenomenon of missing electrons,” Allan said. Above the temperature at which cuprates become superconducting, some of their electrons seem to vanish—measurements find far fewer of them at the energies where they should be. “Everybody knows that from different probes, you see that there’s these vanishing electrons, but nobody knows why,” Allan said. Over the decades, physicists have put forward two main competing explanations. The first hypothesizes that the electrons get stuck in some sort of structure. In this view, electrons freeze into patterns, such as charge density waves, and superconductivity develops separately. The alternative explanation tightly links this phenomenon to superconductivity. “Other people think they’re missing because they form pairs,” Allan said. Pairs of electrons, called Cooper pairs, are what carry current in a superconductor. If, as the material cools, these pairs appear before the material becomes superconducting, they would be a precondition of superconductivity, a waiting room of sorts, where pairs exist but can’t yet move together in lockstep. Settling this proved extremely hard. “Lots of different experimental signatures are kind of lumped inside this,” Allan said. Different instruments all see something, but their readings, Allan said, are never conclusive. “It’s always something kind of incoherent, hard to grasp,” he said. What the researchers needed was a way to see pairs directly, and Allan and his colleagues figured it out. The team decided to listen to shot noise—tiny fluctuations caused by the arrival of individual charges that form the stream of an electric current. Allan says it resembles rain drumming on the roof.  “If you imagine that you have raindrops and then all of a sudden you make the drops twice as heavy, there’ll be more noise,” Allan said. “With the electrons it’s similar. If you have two electrons coming always together, because they’re associated with pairing, then it will be more noise.” The researchers did this measurement with a scanning tunneling microscope, which passed current between a needle-sharp tip and a cuprate sample, letting them listen to the noise at specific energy levels and different locations on the surface. In conventional superconductors, pairing produces a clean doubling of the noise. In cuprates, the signal is much weaker, because single electrons keep flowing alongside the pairs. Making the instrument sensitive enough to discern the pairs took years. “What it took is very talented and persistent students who worked for, I don’t know, four years on this to get it working,” Allan said. “There was not a single trick. It was electronics craftsmanship.” The team examined crystals of a cuprate known as BSCCO, cooled to a few degrees above absolute zero, and compared the noise to the energies at which electrons go missing. By adjusting the voltage between the tip of the needle and the sample, the team could choose the energy of the electrons they were listening to, a bit like turning a radio dial. If the pseudogap came purely from frozen patterns, the team reasoned, the extra noise of pairing should only kick in at energy levels associated with superconductivity. Instead, pairing showed up right at the pseudogap energy. “The fact that there is more noise indicates that pairing is associated with this large gap,” Allan said. The pseudogap’s energy varies from spot to spot on the surface, but wherever the team listened, the extra noise always started rising at the local pseudogap energy. The same pattern held in samples with different chemical compositions. Ruling out other sources of noise, such as atoms shifting on the surface, took a lot of effort. “Noise is super hard to interpret, and that was basically the thing that we had the most sleepless nights about,” Allan said. The decisive check involved placing the samples in magnetic fields of up to six tesla, which suppresses pairing while leaving the overall pattern of missing electrons largely intact. As the magnetic field grew, the noise dropped back toward the single-electron level, exactly as expected if pairs were behind it. The strength of electron pairing can be expressed as the energy required to pull a pair apart. Lowering the temperature helps boost pairing because the warmer a material becomes, the more violently its particles jostle and the more pairs get broken. In conventional superconductors, two ingredients needed for superconductivity—pair formation and phase coherence—occur simultaneously at the critical temperature. (Phase coherence is where the electrons start moving together as a fluid.) In cuprates, Allan and Swart’s study points to a different sequence. The pairing energy the team measured is well above the temperature at which the material superconducts. “So the picture that emerges is that you first have pre-formed pairs, and then only at lower temperatures, phase coherence sets in,” Swart said. That suggests the superconductivity bottleneck is not getting electrons to pair, but getting the pairs to move together. “If you have pairing at such high energies, that means that you might be able to push Tc [superconducting temperature] higher,” Allan said. “This is something people hoped for in the very early days of cuprates, and now everybody’s afraid to even mention that. But to me it gives hope.” An optimistic reading of the results is that cuprate electrons could theoretically pair at room temperature, and we only need to make them coherent to achieve a room-temperature superconductor. The team, though, thinks that’s a bit naive. “I think Ingmar and I both believe that it’s not going to be that simple, because you have all kinds of other effects that are coupled there,” Allan said. The researchers also stress that their experiment does not fully answer the central question. It rules out the idea that the pseudogap comes from frozen patterns alone, but not that such patterns are intertwined with pairing. “Whatever the detailed explanation of the pseudogap is, pairing should be part of it,” Swart said. Because the technique used in the study loses sensitivity as temperature rises, the team couldn’t yet listen above the superconducting temperature, where the pseudogap is usually defined. “Hopefully other groups will do that,” Allan said. “What limits Tc, especially if the energy of pairing is high? What happens at Tc? That’s for me the most interesting question.” Swart wants to make noise maps of the surface with higher resolution and, eventually, an instrument that can detect coherence directly, which he hopes should give us separate markers for the onset of phase coherence and electron pairing. Allan, meanwhile, increasingly thinks the route to better superconductors runs through chemistry, aided by AI, rather than pure physics. “When it comes to trying to make the superconducting material better and increase Tc, I have more hope in direct trial and error,” he said.
Ars Technica - All content
The Bantu expansion changed Africa. Scientists are still tracing how.
2026-11-07

The Bantu expansion changed Africa. Scientists are still tracing how.

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
It’s not easy to extract iron from its ore. But the peoples of the ancient city of Great Zimbabwe—the capital of a thriving kingdom in southern Africa between 1000 to 1700 AD—were masters. At sites near the city’s stone ruins, clusters of iron-smelting furnaces survive today as mounds of rubble. At Mashava, a village 25 miles northwest, fragments bear fingermarks of those who long ago smoothed the structures’ clay interiors. Some of the furnaces were nearly an adult’s arm span in width, with walls thick and strong to contain the heat of more than 2,100° Fahrenheit needed to smelt iron. Unlike other furnaces that required workers to force air into them with bellows, these were designed to suck air in, then out through a chimney, no human labor required. This would have made it significantly easier to produce the iron, which was fashioned into hoes, axes, and arrowheads. “The number and size of these furnaces, together with the use of natural-draft technology, indicate intensive and technologically sophisticated iron production,” says archaeometallurgist Ezekiel Mtetwa of Chicago’s Field Museum of Natural History, who has long studied the furnaces. Scientists are extremely interested in the peoples who created this and other iron-powered societies across eastern and southern Africa. Research points to an adaptable and innovative group from western Africa as the masterminds. Known as Bantu speakers, these communities began gradually expanding across the continent’s southern half, starting from 6,000 to 4,000 years ago. In doing so, they introduced a fundamentally new, sedentary way of life—characterized by farming, raising livestock, pottery-making, and metalworking—to regions previously dominated by hunter-gatherer communities. This migration transformed not only the region’s history and technology, but also its languages: Today, around 350 million Africans speak one of roughly 500 distinct Bantu tongues, from southern African Zulu to eastern African Swahili. And at least as many Africans—as well as many African Americans and Afro-Latinos—carry Bantu ancestry in their DNA. Reconstructing this expansion has been a tough puzzle, especially given the region’s sparse written historical records and limited research funding. But a unique blend of scientists—linguists, archaeologists, and geneticists—have teamed up to unearth clues about the deep origins of the Bantu-speaking people, their path across Africa, and their relationships with other cultures. Although much debate and uncertainty remain, little by little the work is uncovering one of history’s largest and longest human migrations. “It completely reshaped the demography in sub-Saharan Africa, and we are really interested in trying to understand it,” says population geneticist Sandra Beleza of the United Kingdom’s University of Leicester. The first evidence of the Bantu expansion came from observations of similarities among Bantu languages. They’re so alike, in fact, that when the Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama sailed around the southern tip of Africa in 1497, a Portuguese crew member who had spent time in west-central Africa could reportedly communicate with locals in southeastern Africa. Linguists working in the 19th century started referring to the language family as Bantu, the plural of muntu, meaning “human being” in many Bantu tongues. Later linguists reasoned that the languages stemmed from an ancestral people who migrated across the continent from western Africa, which harbors the greatest diversity of Bantu languages (as would be expected for where the languages originated). Soon, archaeologists found supporting evidence: across eastern and southern Africa, pieces of pottery of similar yet diverse styles, alongside first-time traces of village life, metal-making, domesticated animals, and western African crops—all developed within the last 2,000 years. Northern sites usually dated back to earlier time periods than sites farther south. “You can clearly see a north-to-south progression,” says archaeological scientist Shadreck Chirikure of the University of Oxford. By the 1990s, however, some scholars began to question the idea that Bantu languages and skills spread through a migration. Couldn’t these cultural habits have spread while people stayed in the same place, with hunter gatherer communities passing along new technologies, languages, and habits to each other? Genetic techniques that could analyze people’s ancestry helped to resolve that debate. All of us share DNA with our relatives: 50 percent on average with siblings, 12.5 percent with cousins, 3.3 percent with second cousins, and so on. Though these proportions become minute the further back in time a shared common ancestor lived, statistical techniques can tease out patterns. Sure enough, a 2009 genetic analysis of 2,432 Africans found that Bantu-speaking people from western, central, and southern Africa are much more closely related than one might expect given their geographic distance. And in 2024, a team of scientists analyzed the genomes of 1,763 Africans and found the greatest genetic diversity among Bantu speakers in western Africa. As distance from the region increased, diversity declined. “This is a typical signature of a population expanding from one place to another,” explains coauthor Carina Schlebusch, a geneticist at Uppsala University in Sweden. But while linguistic and genetic data point to western Africa—particularly the Grassfields region of Cameroon—as the Bantu homeland, conclusive archaeological proof is thin. At the best-studied archaeological site in the Grassfields, a rock shelter called Shum Laka, pottery fragments appear dating from 7,000 to 6,000 years ago and become abundant by 5,000 to 4,000 years ago. Bowls and jars at first decorated simply with incisions and stamps and later bearing the marks of carved or braided tools rolled over wet clay are a possible, yet inconclusive, trace of Bantu ancestors. South of Cameroon, archaeologists have searched for artifacts in one of the Bantu-speaking people’s first destinations: the Congo rainforest. A handful of times between 2010 and 2023, archaeologist Alexandre Livingstone Smith of Belgium’s Royal Museum for Central Africa traveled via boat along the Congo River as part of a team led by linguist Birgit Ricquier. In a number of villages, Smith found pottery shards of a style consistent with that found farther west, dating back to 2,300 to 2,000 years ago. “That would be the potential first Bantu settlers of the forest,” Smith says. Many experts interpret the trails of pottery as evidence that ceramics traditions were brought by Bantu-speaking people all the way from their original home. What’s unclear, however, is when and how Bantu-speaking peoples acquired their farming and metalworking skills—and, in particular, whether they invented iron smelting themselves or learned it from neighboring, iron-extracting cultures. Scientists have been able to deduce the path that Bantu speakers took to eastern and southern Africa. Some scholars have argued that the path split into two before entering the Congo rainforest, with one group heading east while another went south through the forest. But evidence is now mounting that these groups crossed the rainforest together, and only upon reaching its southern fringes roughly 2,500 to 3,500 years ago did they split into east-moving and southward-moving groups. Some of this evidence comes from linguistic studies. Not unlike the evolution of species, languages diverge and transform over time, creating “trees” that linguists can reconstruct from similarities in grammar and vocabulary. In 2015, a group of researchers did this computationally using data from about 400 Bantu languages. They observed a much larger diversity—and deeper splits—among the Bantu languages spoken in the Congo rainforest, suggesting that Bantu-speaking ancestors spent a long time there, allowing their languages to diversify. Languages spoken farther east and farther south appeared to stem from a less diverse subset of this larger group, explains coauthor Koen Bostoen, a linguist at Belgium’s Ghent University. Later linguistic studies, as well as genetic analyses, have reached similar conclusions. In 2023, Beleza and her colleagues compared the genomes of Bantu speakers from Angola—south of the Congo rainforest—with those from the eastern African country of Mozambique and ones from Cameroon, the Bantu homeland. Remarkably, the eastern African samples were more genetically similar to those from Angola, hundreds of miles to the south of Cameroon and south of the rainforest, than to any of the samples from Cameroon. That points to a recent common ancestor in the Angola region—suggesting that the Bantu split into eastern and southern streams only once they reached the rainforest’s southern edge, Beleza explains. By the time the Bantu people commenced their movement east and south, they had already come into their own as a farming, iron-making, livestock-herding, and pottery-making people, Chirikure says: Many ancient Bantu settlements across eastern and southern Africa contain evidence of this Bantu “package.” Key to the Bantu’s success in southern and eastern Africa, experts suspect, was their sedentary lifestyle. This—and their iron tools—would have allowed them to produce more food and grow in number, propelling them to occupy new territories. Their lifestyle affected the landscape as forests turned into farmland, and it also affected the hunter-gatherer communities who were already living there. Some groups were likely crowded out and disappeared, experts suspect, while others were probably pushed to marginal regions, including Indigenous groups such as rainforest hunter-gatherers and Khoisan peoples, who continue to live in central and southern Africa, respectively, to this day. Still, scientists are learning that Bantu peoples had deep relationships with these two groups. In the 2024 genetic analysis, Schlebusch and her colleagues found that some Bantu-speaking groups in central Africa share about an eighth of their DNA with rainforest hunter-gatherers, and some southern African Bantu speakers share a similar amount with Khoisan groups. Clearly, their ancestors once intermixed. Curiously, in the case of the Khoisan, this mixing tended to arise from pairings of Bantu-speaking men with Khoisan women. One 2021 study of southern African Bantu speakers found that the DNA inside their mitochondria—which people inherit only from their mothers—had greater-than-expected proportions of Khoisan DNA. But the Y chromosomes, which come from fathers, had much higher proportions of DNA with Bantu ancestry. Some experts speculate that this pattern could be the result of Bantu men raiding Khoisan women or preferentially marrying Khoisan women for whom the traditional bride prices would have been cheaper. “Whenever there is a power difference… between two groups, it tends to melt down to this,” says coauthor Ananyo Choudhury, a geneticist at the University of Witwatersrand in South Africa. This pattern also left marks on the language. The characteristic clicks of Khoisan languages also occur in southern African Bantu languages Zulu and Khosa. Bostoen suspects that the children of Khoisan-Bantu pairings were, at first, bilingual. Eventually, “they lost their mothers’ languages, but they left a substrate of the mothers’ languages in the Bantu language.” Archaeologists are also studying the relationships between Bantu farmers and their neighbors. While there may have been conflicts over land, the groups also cooperated with and learned from one another, says Décio Muianga, an archaeologist at Eduardo Mondlane University and the research institute Kaleidoscopio in Maputo, Mozambique. He’s been excavating a rock shelter in Mozambique containing thousands of years’ worth of stone tools, wild animal bones, and wild plant remains—telltale signs of hunter-gatherers living at the site. But interestingly, Bantu-style pottery also appears. “My assumption is that the hunter-gatherers were getting these ceramics from the farmers,” Muianga says. “They were interacting, communicating, and exchanging resources.” There is evidence, too, for cultural exchanges: European anthropologists have reported that some Bantu-speaking groups would solicit San hunter-gatherers to conduct traditional rainmaking ceremonies, for example. Bantu social networks eventually extended well beyond Africa. By 1100 to 1600 AD, many of the complex state societies that Bantu speakers built developed trade networks across the Indian Ocean. Archaeological data shows they imported Chinese porcelain and glass beads; in return, they exported gold, ivory, and iron. In fact, a 12th century Muslim cartographer once wrote that iron from parts of southern Africa—likely referring to Great Zimbabwe’s iron-producing hinterlands, Mtetwa suspects—was deemed to be of much higher quality than that from other places like India. Mtetwa has found evidence that iron smelters near Great Zimbabwe would add bone fragments to the furnaces, perhaps as part of a smelting-blessing ritual, but one with scientific merit: This material contains calcium, which can accelerate the separation of iron from the rest of the ore. That may be a testament to the Great Zimbabweans’ iron-smelting skills: “Some materials introduced within ritual or medicinal practices may simultaneously have had technically beneficial effects on the smelt,” says Mtetwa. Though they’ve made great progress in reconstructing the Bantu world, scientists are aware of, and trying to address, the limitations of their research so far. Linguistic studies, for instance, have been a popular tool to reconstruct Bantu history, but Bostoen cautions that very few Bantu languages have been documented thoroughly enough to definitively trace linguistic trees back in time. Linguists may never get a complete dataset, because some old branches of the Bantu language family may have died out. Another limitation of computational Bantu language-family reconstructions is that they’ve usually assumed that languages have evolved in isolation after different Bantu groups separated. But there may have been extensive cross-pollination among languages as different groups met and interacted. In fact, when linguists factored in the possibility of contact among Bantu languages in a 2026 study, they couldn’t reconstruct the diversification patterns described by previous linguists, says coauthor Igor Yanovich of the University of Vienna, who wrote about the challenges of Bantu linguistic studies in the 2025 Annual Review of Linguistics. In his view, linguists should first focus on thoroughly documenting Bantu languages before attempting to use them to understand Bantu history. In hopes of adding new insights, Schlebusch is working to obtain DNA from ancient human remains. By revealing the identity of people who were living in particular places at particular times, this could help to elucidate when and where different Bantu communities moved into, overlapped with, or repopulated areas. “What we see more and more in genetic data is that the movement was probably complex—that it was these ‘spread over spread’ events,” Schlebusch says. Archaeologists, for their part, hope to fill in the vast gaps in the archaeological record of sub-Saharan Africa—by both conducting new excavations and re-examining existing artifact collections for fresh insights. But all this work requires funding for research and laboratories, especially from African governments, says Chirikure; he, like many of his colleagues, has largely relied on funding from Europe, which is limited. Without more funding, “you are not generating fresh ideas, you are not formulating new hypotheses and testing them using some of the cutting-edge science,” he says. Scientists from all three disciplines are hopeful that they will eventually tell the complete story of how an innovative people spread and diversified across sub-Saharan Africa, mixing traditions with those of earlier peoples and of others who still live there, to create myriad cultures, each one a unique blend of food, philosophy, language, music, and traditions. “If you travel from West Africa today all the way to East Africa, you have a unique cultural diversity that is very hard to find in many other parts of the world,” Muianga says. Combined with the legacy of other groups, he says, “this is the result of the Bantu migration.” This story originally appeared in Knowable Magazine. Knowable Magazine, 2026. DOI: 10.1146/knowable-110326-1″ (About DOIs).

Meet Jensen Huang’s son-in-law, a former pro skier and the Nvidia CEO’s ‘consigliere’ who could play a massive role in the company’s succession plan
2026-11-09

Meet Jensen Huang’s son-in-law, a former pro skier and the Nvidia CEO’s ‘consigliere’ who could play a massive role in the company’s succession plan

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
In January, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was asked how he’s managed to lead the company since its 1993 founding. “The secret for being CEO for this long is, one, don’t get fired, and, two, don’t get bored,” he said at an event in Las Vegas. “I don’t know which one comes first. How long [will I continue as CEO]? For as long as I deserve it.” Ten months later, Huang is putting that adage to the test: Over the weekend, he admitted to having an affair with a business executive suspected by U.S. intelligence of acting as an operative for the Chinese government. China’s foreign ministry denied the allegation. As Huang faces increased scrutiny after he admitted to the affair with an alleged spy, he now faces questions about whether he can continue on as the company’s top brass. Nvidia has since launched an independent investigation into its CEO and his romantic relationship with suspected spy Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed International. Megaspeed is a data center and cloud computing company and one of Asia’s largest buyers of Nvidia chips. Nvidia’s board said Sunday that it has no reason to believe national security or company secrets were compromised. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry,” Huang said in a statement on Sunday. “Today’s news will not slow Nvidia. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption,” a company spokesperson told Fortune in a statement. The probe has reinvigorated investors’ concerns about the need to construct a succession plan, which, notably, Huang and Nvidia have not publicly shared. As of Monday morning, Nvidia stock is down more than 6%. Bernstein analysts, in a note published on Sunday, noted it would be unclear who could or would fill Huang’s shoes because the identity of his company is so inextricably linked to his own. “Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO,” they wrote. “He IS Nvidia, clearly sets the long term strategy and direction, and (with supposedly 60 direct reports) pulls most of the company’s strings himself.” Huang’s two children, Madison and Spencer Huang, both have roles in the company, but Huang’s son-in-law Nico Caprez, Nvidia’s 35-year-old vice president of global AI infrastructure growth, could be among the next in line to lead the AI juggernaut. “I do imagine Huang is kind of preparing [Madison] and Nico for that next step,” one Nvidia employee who works with Caprez, told The Information last month. Caprez is a former alpine skier representing Switzerland who began his career as a Boston Consulting Group consultant. He graduated with an MBA from the London Business School in 2021, where he met Madison Huang. The two married this September. While Madison serves as a senior director of physical AI and robotics product marketing, and Spencer is a director of robotics product management, Caprez rose through the ranks at Nvidia to lead global AI infrastructure growth in less than three years, starting as a corporate development manager in February 2024 before becoming the director of global AI infrastructure enablement for Nvidia’s cloud partners about a year ago. The young executive was part of deal-making efforts to raise $500 billion with six major asset managers including Apollo Global Management and Blackstone, to finance Nvidia chips for data centers in August. Caprez also reportedly assisted in Nvidia’s efforts to take minority stakes in two data center developers as part of its AI infrastructure buildout. Senior leadership has extolled Caprez’s value both within the company and to the CEO more specifically. Vladimir Troy, Nvidia’s vice president of AI infrastructure who works alongside Caprez, said “Nico is very important.” A cloud executive who also met with Caprez called him “Jensen’s consigliere” in an interview with The Information. Huang, however, has been mum on who will lead the company in its next era, whenever that may be. In an interview with Bloomberg last month, the CEO shied away from speculation his daughter would one day replace him at Nvidia’s helm. “I don’t hope the job on her, and the reason for that is because if that’s their aspiration, Spencer of Madison’s, they have to go earn it, and it’s really, really hard work,” he said. “For many people, they can focus on their job and their families. When you’re the CEO of the company, you have to focus on, of course, your family. But I’ve got a whole bunch of other families I’m caring for. I’m responsible for an entire industry, and so I think the pressure of being the CEO is high.” Huang’s newly precarious spot at the top of his company has some worries not just about who one day replaces him, but whether anyone really can. “The so-called ‘key-man risk’ looms large here, and it’s doubtful markets have adequately priced it in,” economist Mohamed A. El-Erian wrote in a post on X. “This will not be easy for the board to navigate.” Daniel Newman, CEO of global technology research firm the Futurum Group, said that what’s made Huang so difficult to replace is that the company has been built around his leadership style, which leans autocratic. “He really drives everything that happens. He has a team of lieutenants that are in charge of making sure that what he asks becomes gospel, that it’s executed ferociously, maniacally,” Newman told Fortune. “The market has built such admiration and confidence for him that he’s become a Jobs or a Musk type of figure at Nvidia, where there just isn’t really any thought of it not being a founder-led company.” Whenever Nvidia transitions to new leadership, it will likely see a shift in strategy, moving slightly away from the rapid clip of innovation Huang has pushed, to becoming an “execution machine” that has solidified backend and supply chain uncertainties to deliver efficiently to partners, Newman added. Changes in the company focus also mean that even if there’s one CEO, there will likely be a leadership team, not a single figure, making Nvidia’s biggest decisions. “It’s very improbable that they’re going to find one person that’s going to fill all the gaps,” Newman said. New York’s graying workforce: Residents over 65 are holding onto jobs longer and driving healthcare to become one of the ‘fastest-growing’ sectors
2026-11-09

New York’s graying workforce: Residents over 65 are holding onto jobs longer and driving healthcare to become one of the ‘fastest-growing’ sectors

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Workers from across the country have long flocked to New York to chase dream jobs and lock down six-figure salaries—but now the opportunity landscape has changed. Professionals have to compete for a limited number of roles, and baby boomers are holding on tight to jobs that might otherwise be up for grabs. A new report from New York Gov. Kathy Hochul’s office reveals that the state continues to struggle with strained hiring and cautious business expansion in an uncertain economy. Total statewide employment is estimated to grow only 0.2% in 2026 and 0.3% in 2027, after rising 1% the year before. Hiring will remain sluggish through next year, the report forecasts, and workforce growth will be subdued because of New York’s shrinking, graying population. “The growth of the State’s labor supply will be limited due to constraints in population growth and the aging workforce,” the report notes. “These trends will likely restrict employment growth, but unemployment rates should remain stable.” Currently, 19.3% of New York’s population is 65 and older—up from 15% a decade ago—according to Hochul’s mid-year report. These baby boomers are intensifying the need for social and healthcare services, in-demand industries that have been battling severe labor shortages across the country. On top of that, America’s oldest working cohort is delaying retirement to make ends meet; a 2022 study from New York’s Office of Budget Policy and Analysis found that residents aged 65 and up were the only group that showed significant labor growth over the 10 years. Older Americans are driving demand for workers in certain industries while potentially delaying the vacancies younger professionals would compete to fill. “Demographic shifts are driving demand for health care and social assistance, making this the fastest growing sector in the labor market,” the recent report explains. “Anticipated shifts in Federal healthcare funding raise concerns about the sector’s ability to sustain employment growth. Meanwhile, job growth in leisure and hospitality, manufacturing, and construction is expected to remain weak due to high interest rates and a slowing economy.” Now, New York is struggling to expand its pool of available workers even as businesses pull back on hiring. The state’s workforce was 1% smaller in 2021 than it was a decade earlier, compared to a nationwide increase of 5.1% during the same period, according to the 2022 report. And the combination of a shrinking labor pool and an increasingly older workforce isn’t unique to New York; across the U.S., baby boomers are staying on the job well past traditional retirement age, reshaping what it means to leave the workforce for good. The number of those who are working past 65 has quadrupled in the U.S. since the 1980s, according to 2023 analysis from Pew Research Center, with around a fifth of the older generation holding down their jobs. Beyond using employer-provided healthcare for as long as possible and keeping their schedules full, Americans are working longer because they worry their savings could run dry. Over two in five retired Americans, about 20 million people, worry their funds won’t support their ideal retirement lifestyle, according to a 2025 survey from investment banking firm D.A. Davidson. Now, Americans plan to work for four years longer than they’d wish to keep their heads above water amid a high cost-of-living and surging healthcare expenses, according to 2026 research from Economist Enterprise. “I think that the days of retirement being a one-time, one-way exit and then you’re done are over,” Elizabeth White, author of 55, Underemployed, told Fortune in 2024. For some who have already called it quits on their careers, that fear has already become a reality. Nearly 20% of American retirees are “struggling” or “living the nightmare,” according to a 2025 study from Schroders. Meanwhile, only 5% said they were “living the dream.” Now, the price of clocking out for good is in the millions. When BlackRock, the world’s largest asset management firm, surveyed U.S. voters earlier this year on how much they think they’d need to retire comfortably, the average response landed around $2.1 million. And Larry Fink, the company’s CEO, said that “almost no one” is close to reaching that goal; BlackRock’s study found that 62% of respondents had less than $150,000 saved for retirement—about 7% of the $2.1 million they think they’d need to stop working. Fink also pointed out that the U.S.’s climbing senior care costs and lack of preparedness for rising life expectancy is exacerbating America’s retirement savings problem. Emma Burleigh is a reporter at Fortune, covering success, careers, entrepreneurship, and personal finance. Before joining the Success desk, she co-authored Fortune’s CHRO Daily newsletter, extensively covering the workplace and the future of jobs. Emma has also written for publications including the Observer and The China Project, publishing long-form stories on culture, entertainment, and geopolitics. Meet the 23-year-old college dropout already making six figures as Walmart’s youngest U.S. store manager
2026-11-09

Meet the 23-year-old college dropout already making six figures as Walmart’s youngest U.S. store manager

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Kiley Pulley is already living a version of the Gen Z dream. At 23, she earns more than six figures, has bought her first home, and is planning a wedding she hopes to pay for without taking on debt. But she isn’t an AI startup founder or an heir to a family fortune—she’s built her career at Walmart and is now the youngest U.S. store manager. Pulley first joined Walmart at 16 as a part-time cashier, following in the footsteps of her mother and sister, who also worked for the retail giant. At the time, she was simply a high school student looking to earn some extra spending money. Her climb up the ranks began two years later, when she was promoted to Academy Trainer, a role responsible for training and developing new employees. But the Arizona native had just started her first semester at Grand Canyon University and initially wasn’t interested in turning her part-time job into a career. Six months later, however, she accepted a promotion to Team Lead—a move that changed how she viewed her future at Walmart. “When I got Team Lead, it really stuck with me that Walmart is something I can make a career out of and something I want to continue to grow in,” Pulley told the Phoenix Business Journal. “That’s when I started applying for salary positions.” By 19, she became an operations manager, and two years later, Pulley became store manager, making her the youngest person to hold the position at any U.S. Walmart store. Now 23, she still holds that distinction, leading a Neighborhood Market outside Phoenix. Pulley told Business Insider that her approach to career growth was relatively simple: show up every day ready to learn something new—and make herself available for opportunities beyond her job description. She regularly participated in calls with Walmart leaders, including monthly discussions with her market manager, and became involved in succession planning. As she moved into the operations manager role, she also took on projects across her market to prepare herself for the next step. Pulley even volunteered to cover for store managers on their days off to gain the experience that was needed to be promoted. Ultimately, that willingness to seek out additional responsibility helped her build relationships with senior leaders and develop the skills needed to advance. “Kiley has embodied ambition, dedication and perseverance since I first met her six years ago as an hourly associate,” Ray Shouse, Walmart’s Phoenix market manager, told the PBJ. “Throughout her career, she has consistently sought out mentors and development opportunities to strengthen her leadership.” But her ambitions haven’t stopped with store manager, either. Pulley is back in school with the help of Walmart’s tuition-assistance program and hopes to be promoted to a market manager by 27 or 28—and eventually a regional executive vice president. As Pulley continues climbing Walmart’s ranks, she’s following a path that has taken others to the top of the Bentonville, Arkansas-based retailer. Walmart CEO John Furner started out as a part-time hourly associate in the company’s garden center in 1993. He eventually worked his way up to store manager before moving into merchandising roles across Walmart and Sam’s Club. One of the most important qualities any leader can have, he’s said, is ability to embrace change. “As long as I’ve been in retail—it’s officially 33 years—it’s always been changing,” Furner said in an interview with Fast Company earlier this year. “Getting comfortable with change in a world that continues to change, I think, is a really great trait for any leader.” Furner’s predecessor, Doug McMillon, also worked his way up from an entry-level position, starting as a warehouse associate unloading trucks before serving as CEO from February 2014 to January 2026. Like Pulley, he said one of his secrets to climbing the ladder was volunteering for work that was on the horizon for his next promotion. “One of the reasons that I got the opportunities that I got was that I would raise my hand when my boss was out of town and he or she was visiting stores or something,” he recalled in a 2024 interview with Stratechery. “I then put myself in an environment where I became a low-risk promotion because people had already seen me do the job,” McMillon added. Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
