Sharebite feeds hundreds of companies. Its CEO wants US employers to copy Brazil
2026-11-09

Sharebite feeds hundreds of companies. Its CEO wants US employers to copy Brazil

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Dilip Rao spent the 2026 midterm campaign listening for someone to talk about hunger. He says almost no one did. “You read these stats about how 48 million Americans or 50 million Americans live in food-insecure households. That’s the reported number!” said Rao, CEO and co-founder of Sharebite, a New York–based employee meal-benefits platform. He argues the real figure is roughly three times higher. “So you’re talking about close to 150 million people. Nearly half the country’s population is struggling; they’re living from paycheck-to-paycheck, and they don’t know where their next meals are coming from,” he said. That number is Rao’s estimate. The official baseline is 47.9 million people who experienced food insecurity in 2024, according to Feeding America’s analysis of federal data. It may be the last reliable count for some time, because the USDA has stopped collecting the household survey behind it. The pressure behind those numbers is showing up at work. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics, and food prices are up 33% since 2019, per an Associated Press analysis of federal data cited by NPR. A Government Accountability Office report found that Uber, Lyft and DoorDash now lead major employers in workers receiving SNAP benefits, ahead of Walmart and McDonald’s. Rao’s pitch is that companies can close part of that gap through something many already pay for: lunch. “We just have to start thinking about hunger, not only as a societal problem but more as an economic problem that businesses can help solve,” he said. Founded in 2016, Sharebite is built for businesses, not consumers. Companies use it to manage employee food ordering, meal allowances, and catering for in-office, remote, and hybrid teams, setting stipends and spending limits from one dashboard instead of processing expense reports. For every meal ordered on the platform, Sharebite donates an equivalent meal through nonprofit partners including Feeding America and City Harvest. The company says it has donated more than 22.4 million meals to date. Its investors include Prosus, Contour Venture Partners, Essential Capital, Lafayette Square, Liberty City Ventures, and Fiserv, the payments company on the 2026 Fortune 500. Sharebite raised a $39 million Series B round in 2022. Rao, a former Credit Suisse investment banker, says the idea came from watching how other countries tackle food insecurity by “mobilizing private sectors to step in and help solve it.” He felt it was “high time for us to be able to implement something like that.” He describes the company as his form of civic service, arguing that serving society, building a successful business, and drawing on the private sector are “all interwoven together.” Rao’s favorite model is Brazil’s Workers’ Food Program, created in 1976. Companies that join receive tax incentives for feeding their employees, and the program now reaches about 22 million workers, according to Brazil’s Ministry of Labor and Employment. “Meal benefits or meal vouchers are a standard practice in Brazil as a result,” Rao said. He argues the U.S. should add food to the employer benefits framework that already covers health care and retirement, a change he says would support workers and stimulate small-business activity. Rao, who has written commentary for Fortune, made a similar case in a 2023 essay on hunger among American workers and again in a 2025 piece written as the record government shutdown ended. The business argument rests partly on cost. Healthcare spending and lost productivity tied to poor diets and food insecurity exceed $1.1 trillion a year, according to a fact sheet from the Food is Medicine Institute at Tufts University. Lunch has also become a lever in the return-to-office. Workers surveyed by Owl Labs said they would come back if employers covered commuting, childcare, and lunch and coffee, Fortune reported in 2023. Rao says Sharebite’s job is aligning “a real business need with a much broader problem,” and credits his team with developing “solutions to solve those problems at that scale” as it works with larger customers. Rao says demand is accelerating. “Last week was our best week ever in the company’s history. October was a record month for us. And it’s the same people who’ve been here for the last few years. Our headcount is small, but our business continues to grow,” he said. He added that the business is more than 30 times larger than it was four-plus years ago. After roughly a decade “building inside this category,” Rao doesn’t think the market has matured. In his view, it is “still at the early beginnings of this entire category.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Exclusive: Publisher Spines raises $64 million in new funding to accelerate effort to automate book publishing with AI
2026-11-09

Exclusive: Publisher Spines raises $64 million in new funding to accelerate effort to automate book publishing with AI

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Spines, a startup that uses AI to help authors publish their books, including using AI agents to edit manuscripts, design covers, translate text, market the published book, and manage their social media campaigns, has secured $64 million in new capital, the company said Monday.The company, which has offices in Israel and New York, is what is known as a “hybrid publisher.” Hybrid publishers largely work with authors who are unable to obtain a traditional publishing deal from a commercial publishing house or who wish to retain more control and receive a larger royalty split than an author typically would get with a traditional publishing house. As with self-publishing, authors pay hybrid publishers an upfront fee to publish their book. Hybrid publishers also take a cut of an author’s sales as royalties. But hybrid publishers provide a greater level of service and support to authors than is the case with self-publishing.The funding package for Spines includes a $29 million Series A-1 equity round led by Zeev Ventures, with participation from Aleph, NordStar, M-Fund, LionTree, and angel investor Dan Senor. Spines is also receiving $35 million in what it calls “a non-dilutive growth financing facility” from Twelve, a credit-focused investment management firm.The company declined to disclose its valuation after the funding. Spines is also announcing that its new AI-powered services have helped it hit a $50 million annual revenue run rate, representing 400% year-over-year growth. The company has published more than 8,000 authors to date, accounting for some 12,000 titles, founder and CEO Yehuda Niv told Fortune. He said roughly 400 new writers sign up for Spines’ service each month.About 15 years ago, while partway through a master’s degree in electronic engineering, Niv published a book of his own and concluded that “the publishing process is completely broken.” That bad experience motivated Niv to get into publishing himself. He bootstrapped Niv Publishing, which he says became Israel’s largest publishing house, putting out about 1,200 titles a year. Roughly six years ago, he said, he realized that AI would eventually “make every [traditional] publishing company on the planet irrelevant.”In response, in 2021 Niv founded Spines, which describes itself as an “operating system for authors.” Writers upload a finished manuscript, and specialized agents handle editing, proofreading, formatting, cover design, metadata, ebook and print-on-demand files, and distribution to more than 100 channels, including Amazon, Barnes & Noble, and Baker & Taylor. Authors can produce an audiobook with a synthetic narrator or a clone of their own voice, and translate the book—and that audiobook—into other languages. Spines currently offers seven target languages and plans to reach 20 within six months. After publication, the platform tracks royalties and runs marketing campaigns. Niv argues the agents can do some jobs better than the freelancers authors typically hire. Of the roughly 10,000 authors he published in Israel, he said, every one had the same problem: “The cover designer didn’t have time to read my book.” Spines’ cover agent reads the manuscript, researches which retail categories give the book its best shot, which designs work best in those categories, and designs the cover to match those metrics. AI agents accelerate the time it takes to get from manuscript to having a book in stores, a process that in traditional publishing takes many months to more than a year, bringing it down to just weeks. Spines’ publishing packages start at about $3,000 and run to $20,000 for a premium tier that publishes a book in three languages, each with an audiobook; most authors pick a mid-tier bundle costing $5,000 to $7,000, Niv said. Spines recently changed its royalty split so that authors keep 80% of net royalties, up from 70%. The company reports royalties monthly, rather than the twice-yearly or annual statements common in the industry, and is working toward weekly and eventually real-time reporting, Niv said. Niv said that Spines only publishes books “100% written by humans” and that it uses software to try to weed out AI-generated manuscripts. “We are not here to replace [authors] with AI,” he said, insisting that Spines exists to “empower” human writers.Still, some aspects of Spines’ use of AI may raise copyright issues, particularly around AI-designed book covers. U.S. courts and the Copyright Office have held that images made entirely by AI can’t be copyrighted. Niv said Spines licenses the image-generation tools it uses and passes a license on to authors. He argued that is often a broader license than what authors get from human illustrators, who usually keep the copyright and license the image only for the book cover. Spines has roughly 350 employees, Niv said, adding that only about 50 work in R&D; most of the rest handle customer success and support, helping authors with decisions that he says software can’t make for them. “Our AI agent designed for you three amazing covers, but eventually you have to choose one,” he said. “For an author, that’s an emotional decision.” Spines’ human marketing experts are there to help. For now Spines sells its platform directly to authors who don’t have a traditional publisher. But Niv said the company is in early talks with one of the Big Five U.S. publishers about a partnership in which Spines and its AI agents would be used to help run that publishing house’s services. “Spines has all the ingredients to become the category leader in the next generation of publishing,” Oren Zeev, the founding partner of Zeev Ventures, said in a statement. Niv said Spines will use the debt facility to fund growth and the equity to build out its product and expand beyond the U.S. into other English-speaking markets, including the U.K., Canada, and Australia. He said he is aiming for 200% to 300% annual growth. Most of the company’s staff work in Eastern Europe, the U.S., and Latin America, but R&D and management are based in Herzliya, Israel. Jeremy Kahn is the AI editor at Fortune, spearheading the publication's coverage of artificial intelligence. He also co-authors Eye on AI, Fortune’s flagship AI newsletter. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
I told women to stop chasing balance. Now I’m telling them to ask for help
2026-11-08

I told women to stop chasing balance. Now I’m telling them to ask for help

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
Alyssa Jaffee is a partner at 7wire Ventures, a venture capital firm investing in digital health companies. She holds board roles at NOCD, MedArrive, Brightline, Zerigo Health, Transcarent, WellTheory and the National Venture Capital Association. I welcomed my fourth baby on June 29, 2026, six days after writing this. These words capture the week before, when I was 38 weeks pregnant, and the question that kept me awake still feels urgent now. It was 11 p.m. I was 38 weeks pregnant with my fourth child, and instead of sleeping I was lying in the dark, silently running the list. Camp forms. The pediatrician follow-up. The board deck due to review. Who’s covering carpool the week I deliver? My body was begging me to rest, and my brain wouldn’t let it. By every external measure, I was doing it all. But lately, I could feel myself disappearing. Four years ago, I wrote an article urging women to stop asking about work-life balance. My advice? You can never be truly balanced. The goal is to live unbalanced, to teeter back and forth, but with conviction about which way you’re leaning. I was pregnant with my third child then, a hustling venture capitalist. Today, I am a mother of four, a growing portfolio behind me, and over three-quarters of a billion dollars raised to deploy into healthcare companies. Now I find myself reflecting on the advice I gave back then. The question I keep asking: did we perfect the art of teetering so well that we forgot to live? Four kids and a full career later, the list of to-dos is endless. Check one off, move on to the next. Whack-a-mole to the extreme. Focus on optimization. Prioritize. BE EFFICIENT. Get the to-do list done. Don’t worry if I’m the sacrifice. The mental load has changed from our parents’ generation. Gone are the days when kids ran free until dinner. Unencumbered freedom has been exchanged for meticulous planning. Our kids are overscheduled, and we bear the brunt. And it’s not just the kids. It’s our jobs. The expectation of how much to produce, how high to climb. “How does she do it all?” And the work never clocks out anymore. Hybrid and remote have blurred the line between the office and the kitchen table until there was no line at all. Now AI promises to make us faster, which somehow just means we’re expected to produce more, around the clock, forever. But is this having it all? Countless working moms tell me stories of their day, collapsing into bed with just barely enough energy to make it to the following day. We figured out how to live in the unbalance yet it appears we teetered too much, too quickly, and we started losing ourselves in the process. The trad wife movement has gained steam in part for this reason, a desire for simplicity. It’s not really about sourdough and aprons; it’s a referendum on a deal that stopped working. When “do it all” leaves you this depleted, opting out entirely starts to look like the only way to breathe. My work as an investor in healthcare companies serving women makes it impossible to ignore: the mental load is exhausting, and it’s measurably hurting women’s health. Without our health, we have nothing, and women are paying for it. Mothers carry 71% of the household’s mental load, the invisible work of remembering, planning, anticipating, despite the fact that fathers are more involved than at any point in modern history. They’ve nearly tripled their childcare hours since the 1960s, yet it still isn’t enough. And that load has a body count. There’s a name for what chronic stress does to a body: allostatic load, the cumulative wear and tear of a stress response that never fully switches off. It’s not a metaphor. It shows up as inflammation, hypertension, and disease, and it’s quietly accruing in women who are praised for how much they can carry. Women spend 25% more of their lives in poor health than men, roughly nine years each. Nearly half of working women say their job has damaged their mental health in just the past six months. This isn’t fatigue. It’s a deterioration we’ve normalized. The system that’s supposed to support us isn’t built to. Less than 9% of NIH research dollars over the last decade went to women’s health. Humans were supposed to be raised in tribes, in community, yet somehow today we are expected to accomplish more than we ever have, in isolation. Please your spouse, take care of your kids, crush it at work, don’t ask for help. In fact, don’t even tell anyone you have help. A friend of mine was on a panel and was asked how she had four kids. She replied honestly, “I have two nannies and lots of help.” Later she was scolded by the conference organizers, “shhh don’t tell the truth! We don’t want to scare people.” But isn’t asking for help one of the most important things we can do for ourselves and each other? Somehow, we have lost the ability to be honest and say “I can’t do this alone. I need you.” I felt that loss too. The optimization of my life was consuming me to the point I lost the greatest gift of all: time. So, I sought out help. First in small, confined ways, and later, more outwardly. It’s still incredibly difficult. I’m a high-performing woman with local family and resources to pay for help; shouldn’t I be able to do it all? In the pandemic, I found myself on a text chain with four other women. Half of us had never met in person. Six years later, we’ve texted every single day since, and I consider them some of my closest confidants. You want to know what we named our chat? The Womb of Silence. It’s where one of us admits she’s drowning before a board meeting. Where someone asks, with no shame, if anyone has a pediatrician they trust, a therapist taking new patients, a name for the thing they’re feeling or ailment they’re experiencing. Where “I can’t do this alone” is the price of entry, not a confession. Nothing you say there gets you exposed. Nothing gets you judged. You can always ask for help, and someone always answers. I’ve since replicated this feeling with other groups, my Core of Core and Bad Ass VC Mamas, to name a few. That chat taught me something the rest of my life kept trying to unteach me: asking isn’t the moment you fail. It’s the moment you actually start living and thriving. Little by little, I am trying to reframe my brain to believe that doing it all myself doesn’t win me any awards. Deteriorating in plain sight won’t improve my health and happiness. Asking for help will. The companies I’m drawn to as an investor are the ones giving women back the two things the world keeps taking: their time and their health. Every time I ask for help, I get a little of myself back. The woman lying awake at 11 p.m., doing it all and quietly disappearing, is starting to reappear. Not because I’m carrying less, but because I’m finally not carrying it alone. Maybe it’s okay ChatGPT wrote the tooth fairy poem or Instacart auto-ordered my groceries or, gasp, I got a babysitter to take a nap. And as I reflect four years later, with my fourth and final baby now here, I realize that maybe it’s okay to just rest on one side of the see-saw. It’s okay even to climb off of it for a bit to take a break. Ask for help when you need it. And we all know, we need it. Silicon Republic
Might AI change how we understand mathematical engagement?
2026-11-09

Might AI change how we understand mathematical engagement?

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Carolin Antos of the University of Konstanz and Balthasar Grabmayr of the University of Tübingen discuss how artificial intelligence is changing the way people engage with mathematical models. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) OpenAI recently announced that one of its AI models solved one of the hardest problems in modern mathematics: the Navier-Stokes problem. The equations at the heart of this problem describe the movement of fluids like water and, according to the scientific definition, air. The Navier-Stokes equations are essential to understanding phenomena such as ocean currents, blood circulation and airflow over airplane wings. Yet mathematicians have struggled for decades to determine whether coherent solutions to these equations exist. It was for this reason that the US-based Clay Mathematics Institute, which is dedicated to disseminating mathematical knowledge, chose the Navier-Stokes problem as one of its seven Millennium Prize Problems. These are the most important and challenging unsolved problems in modern mathematics, with a prize of US$1m offered for the first correct solution to each. A problem that resisted generations of mathematicians thus has apparently been solved by artificial intelligence. While these advancements sent shock waves through the mathematical world, they seem to be only the tip of the iceberg. On 6 October 2026, OpenAI presented its solutions to more than 300 longstanding unsolved problems across most areas of mathematics with their new internal model. Does this mark the end of mathematics as a human endeavour? Answering this question requires a closer look at mathematical proofs. Broadly speaking, a proof is an argument for a mathematical claim that starts from generally accepted assumptions and proceeds through the different steps of reasoning for the claim. Before mathematicians accept such an argument as a proof, they need to check that each step is correct. Computers can increasingly help with this task, but for major AI-generated results, human mathematical supervision and expertise remains indispensable. However, a fully verified proof is not the end of the story. Mathematicians do not only want to know that a theorem is true; they also want to understand why it is true and what can be learned from its proof. Prof Terence Tao, from University of California, Los Angeles, is one of the world’s leading mathematicians and a prominent supporter of AI’s use in mathematics. He recently coined the term “proof digestion” for the many ways that mathematicians continue to work with a proof after its correctness has been established. They may reorganise the proof around more natural concepts, streamline a complicated argument, connect it to existing mathematics or extract methods that can be applied elsewhere. Proof digestion itself can be highly fruitful and produce new mathematics. Even after a proof has been digested, the mathematical work is rarely done. Major mathematical results often become stepping stones towards new open problems. Important variants of the Navier-Stokes problem, for example, cannot be solved by the methods of the recent proof proposed by OpenAI. This is typical of major breakthroughs; their importance often lies not only in answering a long-standing question, but also in introducing novel mathematical concepts and opening up new lines of research. Often, one proof simply is not enough. Mathematicians regularly search for new proofs of results that have already been proven. Take, for example, the Pythagorean Theorem: a²+b²=c². This relates to the three lengths of a right-angled triangle. This result was proved in antiquity, yet a book published in the 1920s collects more than 300 proofs for the Pythagorean Theorem that have been established over the last two millennia. If the only purpose of a proof is to establish that a result is true, all but the earliest of these proofs would be redundant. Instead, the opposite is the case. Different proofs may reveal new insights into why the result is true, uncover new connections to concepts from other mathematical areas or suggest new methods of generalisation. Thus, proofs are not only valued for verifying mathematical results, but also for the explanation and understanding they provide. What exactly makes a proof explanatory or illuminating is difficult to pin down – and remains an ongoing topic in the philosophy of mathematics. This makes explanation and understanding much less clear goals for AI than simply producing a correct proof. The recent advancements in AI will likely transform mathematical practice profoundly. But mathematics is not simply the production of correct answers to unsolved problems. Proofs still need to be understood, reorganised, connected to other results and used to generate new questions. As AI models become ever more effective at producing proofs, these other parts of mathematical research may become increasingly important. In the near future, it may therefore matter less who – or what – can produce a proof than whether this can be used for mathematical progress. Carolin Antos is an assistant professor of philosophy at the University of Konstanz. She works at the interface of philosophy, mathematics and logic. After coming to Konstanz via a Marie-Curie grant, she became a member of the Institute for Advanced Studies at the university. Her work is in the philosophy of mathematics, the philosophy of science and epistemology. Balthasar Grabmayr is a junior professor of philosophy at the University of Tübingen. Before that he was an Azrieli international postdoctoral fellow in the Department of Philosophy at the University of Haifa and concurrently held a postdoctoral position at the Blavatnik School of Computer Science at Tel Aviv University. He received his PhD in philosophy from Humboldt University of Berlin. His research mostly focuses on logic and the philosophies of logic, mathematics and computation. Reuters: EU report finds Russian interference during France riots

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
False narratives ‘fuel fear’ and ‘shape public perceptions ahead of elections’, EU analysis reportedly found. An internal EU analysis, seen by Reuters, has found that a Russian network used social media to sow unrest during last month’s riots in France. According to the report, the Russian network, called ‘Operation ​Overload’, spread disinformation videos on social media impersonating real media outlets during the protests. Some of these videos contained hostile anti-immigrant propaganda, including that migrants had raped protesters. France saw mass protests focused on a range of societal issues last month, gathering as many as 800,000 people at their peak. The protests, though largely peaceful, were accompanied by some rioting and looting. The EU internal assessment looked at the period between 7 October and 21 October. Reuters reported that the EU found evidence of Russian attempts to “depict the French leadership as weak and increasingly authoritarian”. The report found that Operation Overload, active since September 2023, targets Western “public ​figures, institutions and media outlets” ⁠in an attempt to “discredit” them and their support for Ukraine, while promoting Russian interests. State or state-backed propaganda tactics such as these are also called ‘foreign information manipulation and interference’, or FIMI. A separate EU report, published in March, found that Operation Overload utilised AI to manipulate content or fully generate realistic videos impersonating specific individuals. Reuters’ report comes after the French president Emmanuel Macron said last month that there was “no visible foreign interference” during the riots, but added that “there was foreign manipulation of information afterwards”, referencing comments made by Israeli prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu and US president Donald Trump. Reuters also viewed another internal assessment compiled by France’s foreign ‌ministry, which reportedly found that social media accounts linked to Israel and to the US ‘Make America Great Again’ movement also promoted Islamophobia and anti-immigrant narratives during the riots. Such interference activities can “fuel fear and division, and shape public perceptions ahead of elections”, the internal EU document read, according to Reuters. Henkel, grad programmes and a career in sustainability and data

Henkel, grad programmes and a career in sustainability and data

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Henkel’s Giovanna Ragusa discusses her early beginnings in materials engineering and the Chaingers Operations and Supply Chain trainee programme. After starting out in materials engineering, Henkel’s Giovanna Ragusa, who is now a sustainability and data analytics strategy lead, also completed the Chaingers Operations and Supply Chain trainee programme. She told SiliconRepublic.com: “It immediately stood out to me because of the breadth of experiences it offers. “The programme includes hands-on exposure to factory operations, Lean Six Sigma Green Belt certification, leadership and project management training and rotations across different areas of operations and supply chain, such as quality, logistics, engineering, health, safety and environment.” She explained that having the chance to develop a network, grow via a range of experiences and gain exposure by working across different functions and sites globally, gave her an “incredibly valuable learning opportunity”. There is no real ‘typical’ day in the trainee programme, which is one of the things I enjoy most about it. Depending on the project I am working on, my day can include analysing data, spending time on the shop floor to understand processes, having meetings with stakeholders, or managing project activities. Because the programme includes rotations across different departments, I have had the opportunity to work on a wide variety of topics, from process improvements to productivity increase and waste reduction, and collaborate with people from different functions and backgrounds. My favourite part of the job is probably the combination of problem-solving, investigation on root causes of problems and working with people. I enjoy identifying opportunities for improvement, managing projects, helping the team turn ideas into actions and seeing the impact of a project once it is implemented. Definitely. At the beginning of the programme, a lot of the focus is on learning about the company, understanding how the business operates and getting started on our Lean Six Sigma Green Belt project while supporting other initiatives in parallel. As the programme progresses and our knowledge grows, we are given the opportunity to take ownership of more projects and responsibilities. This allows us to broaden our experience and gain exposure to different areas of the business. We work closely with managers across the site on a daily basis, including the site manager, and we also have opportunities to interact with European leaders from different functions. In the final six months of the programme, each trainee takes on a dedicated project sponsored by one of these senior leaders, very often a regional European project rather than site specific. This significantly increases both our level of responsibility and our exposure to senior stakeholders, while we continue supporting projects and activities at our local site. I have developed a wide range of skills since joining the programme. One of the biggest has been taking ownership of projects and being accountable for decisions, actions and results. As trainees, we are given a significant level of responsibility from an early stage, which has really helped me build confidence and an entrepreneurial mindset. I have also strengthened my project management and stakeholder management skills. Many of the projects involve working with different departments, functions and levels of seniority, often with competing priorities and expectations. Learning how to align stakeholders, communicate effectively and keep projects moving forward has been a valuable part of my development. Another important skill I have developed is agility. Since the programme includes rotations across different departments, we are constantly exposed to new teams, processes and ways of working. This requires us to adapt quickly, learn fast, and become comfortable operating in unfamiliar environments. One of the most challenging aspects of the programme has been learning that projects do not always go according to plan. Priorities change, unexpected obstacles arise and sometimes you need to rethink your approach completely. This has taught me the importance of being adaptable, resilient and willing to think outside the box when challenges occur. If there is one thing I wish I had known before starting, it is that this is a normal part of working life. Early on, it can be frustrating when things do not progress as expected, but I have learned that everyone faces changing priorities and setbacks, regardless of their role or level of experience. Embracing that reality from the beginning makes it much easier to stay flexible, keep learning, and move projects forward. 100pc. Looking back at everything I have experienced and learned over the past two years, I would absolutely recommend the programme. It has given me opportunities to work on meaningful projects, learn from people at all levels of the organisation, and develop skills that would have taken much longer to acquire in a traditional entry level role. The programme constantly pushes you out of your comfort zone, which can be challenging at times, but that is also where most of the learning happens. For anyone looking to accelerate their career development and gain exposure to different areas of a business, it is an excellent opportunity. One of the biggest advantages of the programme is the wide range of career paths it can open after completion. Former trainees have moved into areas such as process engineering, project management, digitalisation, lean, production leadership and supply chain, among many others. Through the different rotations and experiences, you gain a better understanding of your strengths, interests and the type of career that best suits you. In my case, I have always been interested in sustainability, which was the focus of my master’s degree in sustainable materials. Combined with the data analysis skills I developed throughout the programme, this led me to my current role in sustainability and data analytics, bringing together two areas I am genuinely passionate about. Be willing to step outside your comfort zone and dare to try things differently. Not every idea will work and not every project will go according to plan, but those experiences often provide the best learning opportunities. Stay curious, be open to learning from others and don’t be afraid to make mistakes. Most importantly, don’t take challenges or setbacks personally. Resilience is essential and being able to push through difficulties, learn from them and keep moving forward will help you get the most out of the programme. FT: EU sues consortium including IBM over border system delays
2026-11-09

FT: EU sues consortium including IBM over border system delays

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
The new entry and exit system for the Schengen region has caused long delays at EU airports. The EU has filed a lawsuit against IBM, French IT group Atos and Italian defence contractor Leonardo over delays in developing its new smart border system which was rolled out earlier this year. The Financial Times (FT) was first to report on the story earlier today (9 November). EU-Lisa is the bloc’s agency that manages large-scale IT systems overseeing entry to and exit from the Europe’s Schengen region, which allows free travel between 29 countries. The new Entry/Exit System (EES), which came into effect in April, requires travellers from outside the Schengen bloc to scan their fingerprints and biometric data when entering the region. It hopes to gradually replace passport stamps with a digital system that can detect overstayers and heighten security. The system was originally intended to launch in 2022, but was fully rolled out only this year. Its introduction has been plagued with issues, creating long queues at airports around Europe and causing passengers to miss flights. According to the FT, EU-Lisa sued the companies last December after the three repeatedly missed deadlines and disputed the agency’s warnings and fines. Meanwhile, a number of EU countries are still in the process of implementing their EES scanners. The European Commission told the publication that the lawsuit relates to delays in the delivery of the new system and is not about its operation since then. SiliconRepublic.com has reached out to IBM for comments. EU-Lisa is headquartered in Estonia with operations in France, a backup site in Austria and a liaison office in Belgium. In a statement to the FT, a spokesperson for Estonia’s Harju county court confirmed the lawsuit’s existence. EU-Lisa told the FT that the legal matter has “no impact on the functioning” of the EES, which it said is “fully operational” across Europe. The publication, which reviewed letters between the consortium of businesses and the agency, reported long-standing disagreements going back years, including around the Covid-19 pandemic, supposedly “unrealistic” deadlines, and payments. Almost half of Irish companies failing to address skills gap, finds Learnovate

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Researchers said that a failure to increase access to learning in line with continued deployment of AI could limit its successful implementation and have wider consequences for the economy. The Learnovate Centre, a Trinity College Dublin-based global research and innovation initiative, has published the results of a report indicating that while organisations in Ireland are increasingly implementing AI into their operations, many are failing to match technological advancements with the required training and development for employees. The centre’s annual Learning Signals Survey collected data from roughly 225 professionals across various sectors, such as education, learning technology and business, among others. What was discovered is that almost half of participating organisations to the research are failing to proactively address skills gaps around AI. The 2026 research showed that the number of companies actively using the technology in their everyday operations rose to 28pc, which is a 5pc increase on the previous year. According to Learnovate, a failure to increase access to learning and development in line with continued deployment of AI could limit its successful implementation and have wider consequences for the economy. Of the Irish organisations that are failing to address skills gaps within their workforce, according to Learnovate, nearly one-quarter are responding reactively based on short-term need, while around one in six (15pc) recognise the gaps but lack the direction to address them. One in 10 do not view skills development as strategically important. One in four reported that their organisation has a regularly updated, forward-looking skills strategy, while 25pc also said that their organisation was in the process of developing a strategy and experimenting with future-focused skills initiatives. Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Join us to start Caring. Connecting. Growing together Life-changing career opportunities for you Learnovate’s research identified a number of barriers to upskilling. Three-quarters of contributors listed time and resource constraints as a significant issue. 60pc of organisations were found to be prioritising competing operational demands over learning and development. Change fatigue (16pc) and a failure by leadership to value learning (7pc) also made the list of obstacles cited by participants. Nessa McEniff, managing director of the Learnovate Centre, said, “The Learnovate Learning Signals Survey 2026 shows that almost half of organisations are not proactively addressing skills gaps, while time and competing operational demands are continuing to squeeze learning and development. “If organisations want to realise the potential of AI, they need to make workforce development part of their AI strategy. Failure to do so carries risks for the organisation, their individual AI implementation projects and the wider economy, as the efficiency benefits of AI risk not being reflected in companies’ bottom line. “AI adoption is accelerating but the survey shows that organisations are not treating skills development as a priority. To maximise the potential of any technology, organisations must have skilled and confident people to use it effectively.” Earlier this year, Learnovate announced the Responsible AI for Learning (RAIL) initiative. Learnovate leads the scheme, which is made up of professionals from all four education domains – including schools, higher education, vocational education and training, and professional education – as well as representatives from the Department of Education, teaching unions and other sectors. The programme aims to enable practitioners in the sharing of knowledge as they interpret guidelines and comply with regulations around AI. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. The Death of the Cybertruck Proves That Elon Musk Can’t Actually Predict the Future

The Death of the Cybertruck Proves That Elon Musk Can’t Actually Predict the Future

Futurism | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
The Tesla Cybertruck is dead — and with it, whatever credibility Elon Musk had as a prognosticator of the future. The angular, heterodox pickup represented the culmination of Musk’s eccentric brand of genius, at least to his legions of followers. It wasn’t supposed to just be an oddball soon-to-be curiosity like the DeLorean, but something that would transform the automotive industry. That future did not come to pass. Last week, Musk announced on X that production of the truck would cease next summer, sending off this iconic symbol of himself with a single ignominious tweet. Musk had previously mythologized his creation as a supposedly “apocalypse-proof” tank that could stop bullets, wade through water, and drive itself. Its stainless steel exterior and low poly design evoked a brash optimism of a future where aerodynamics, and perhaps practicality, were no longer a concern. Most importantly, in the billionaire’s telling, it would sell in incredible numbers. Musk originally predicted that he would move up to 500,000 Cybertrucks every year, before setting a more realistic target — in relative terms, at least — of 250,000. It says a lot that Musk actually put his money where his mouth was, pouring about $2 billion of Tesla funding into the Cybertruck’s development. Musk also built his Gigafactory Texas in part to manufacture hundreds of thousands of the brutalist pickups per year. In the end, Tesla didn’t manage to sell its annual target at any point during the truck’s short-lived lifespan. In the three years it was being built, it sold around just 71,000 Cybertrucks total, estimates show. Experts say it could be the biggest flop in automotive history — even more disastrous than the notorious Ford Edsel. Besides entering an already niche market — how many customers are there for a bizarre-looking electric pickup truck that costs around $100,000, depending on the model? — it didn’t help that a lot of what Musk promised about the truck didn’t turn out to be true. It wasn’t as cheap as the $40,000 price tag Musk originally set. It couldn’t double as a boat, as some drivers learned the hard way. And with 11 recalls under its belt for issues like its stainless steel body panels flying off and reports of it bricking after going through a car wash, it was far-from apocalypse proof. And somehow Musk, in his capital-A Abundance of wisdom, thought it would revolutionize the automotive industry. Remember, this is the same guy promising to not just put data centers in space — something that many experts are skeptical is feasible — but do so in such quantities that it’d amount to building a “sentient sun,” in Musk’s own words. His Grok AI will unlock the “true nature of the universe.” His Starship megarocket will help “extend the light of consciousness to the stars” and “make life multiplanetary.” And of course, humans will be colonizing Mars any day now; two years ago he predicted the first humans would reach the Red Planet in four years. We have two more years to go. In any case, Musk seems to be in denial that his brainchild was a failure, arguing in a Sunday tweet that his winding down of its production was “not a case of [the] Cybertruck being unprofitable.” More on Elon Musk: Governor-Elect of Texas Announces Plans to Bring Elon Musk Under Control

Scientists Stumped by Why Mysterious Object Is Accelerating as a Japanese Spacecraft Rushes to Catch It
2026-11-09

Scientists Stumped by Why Mysterious Object Is Accelerating as a Japanese Spacecraft Rushes to Catch It

Futurism | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
There’s something puzzling about 1998 KY26, a mysterious object that the Japanese space agency’s Hayabusa2 spacecraft is on its way to visit: it’s accelerating. That isn’t necessarily out of the ordinary for asteroids and comets, but it appears to be driven by a propulsion source that scientists have yet to understand, as ScienceAlert reports. One explanation, from Harvard astronomer Avi Loeb, suggests that 1998 KY26 is a derelict Soviet spacecraft that launched almost 40 years ago. More conventional attempts to account for its acceleration proffer that it’s a “dark comet,” a type of space object that acts like a rocky asteroid, but experiences acceleration that can’t be accounted for by gravitational forces. Yet according to new research published in the journal Icarus, it’s too warm to be a dark comet, which can accelerate by releasing ice that sublimates into gas. If that were the case, observations show that it would’ve run out of ice reserves a long time ago. “Our temperature model results indicate that the water outgassing rate on 1998 KY26 would be too high to maintain buried ice, refining the understanding of the asteroid’s composition,” authors of the paper and Georgia Institute of Technology aerospace engineers Korbin Hansen and Masatoshi Hirabayashi wrote. 1998 KY26 is a 36-foot space object that rotates every five and a half minutes and is currently located 186 million miles away from Earth. After modeling its temperature using pre-existing data, Hansen and Hirabayashi found that even its coldest regions measured upwards of -77 degrees Fahrenheit. While that may sound chilly, it’s too warm for stores of ice that could sublimate into gas and provide thrust. At such warm temperatures, 1998 KY26 would’ve run out of fuel in just 62 years, a blink of an eye for an object suspected to be billions of years old, as ScienceAlert points out. The team concludes that “1998 KY26 is likely a dehydrated asteroid,” meaning that it lost its stores of water long ago. Other explanations for its acceleration could be solar radiation or “thermal emission forces” pushing it. Fortunately, Hayabusa2 is expected to rendezvous with the mysterious object in July 2031, which could give scientists a first-hand look at whatever is going on. Trump Admin Caught Illegally Canceling Billion-Dollar Solar Project That Would Have Helped Lower Utility Bills Across the Country

Donald Trump and his Republican lackeys were decimated in this week’s midterm elections, in large part thanks to an ongoing cost of living crisis. According to the broadcaster’s reporting, the Environmental Protection Agency under Trump appointee Lee Zeldin has refused to dish out billions of congressionally-approved funds meant to expand access to solar energy in low-income communities. The Biden-era program, dubbed “Solar For All,” earmarked $7 billion for distribution to solar energy developments led by states and Tribal governments. States like Massachusetts and Michigan were previously approved for $156 million, while others like Arkansas were set to receive over $93 million. But when it came time for the federal government to pay the piper, Zeldin and the EPA refused, calling it a “boondoggle.” That decision set off an immediate legal battle, which was recently resolved in favor of the Solar For All recipients. Despite a federal court order that the government dish out the funds, the EPA has yet to loosen its purse strings, leaving questions as to whether the agency will appeal the decision — or even ignore it entirely. “This is a program that has the potential to lower people’s energy bills, get inexpensive clean electricity on the grid, and help the communities that the Solar For All program was designed to help,” senior attorney at the Conservation Law Foundation James Crowley, who is representing plaintiffs in one of the court cases, told WBUR. Though it might be one of the more ironic examples of Zeldin’s EPA shooting itself in the foot, it’s hardly the first. Zeldin, a former center-right Republican turned MAGA-die hard, has moved aggressively to dismantle dozens of environmental regulations since winning the job, many of which were explicitly designed to protect poor American communities. So while Trump’s side may have lost handily in the midterms, it’s clear his administration’s war on poor and working class Americans is far from over — as the ongoing refusal to release cheap, green energy makes abundantly clear. Climate Change Has Become So Bad That Rice Krispies Don’t Have Actual Rice in Them Anymore

Climate Change Has Become So Bad That Rice Krispies Don’t Have Actual Rice in Them Anymore

Futurism | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
Snap, Crackle, and Pop! The sonic slogan of Rice Krispies is now becoming an anachronism. The classic puffed rice foodstuff now no longer contain actual rice grains, at least in UK and European markets, the manufacturer Kellogg’s recently admitted. It will now use rice flour and corn flour, which aside from gustatory concerns, will result in it being noticeably quieter than it used to be. Kellogg’s, in the face of complaints online, insists that retaining the original sound was a “huge focus.” It’s yet another example of the corporate cost-cutting putrefaction we’ve come to expect — and the reason for it is grim: climate change. A spokesperson told media outlets that the reformulation will help “maintain consistent quality at a time when changing weather conditions are affecting the quality and availability of some of the rice and grains used in our cereals.” The company didn’t get into specifics, but the recipe change comes as a powerful “super El Niño” climate phenomenon has devastated agriculture in South and Southeast Asia, where more than half the world’s rice is grown. Scientists say that the impacts of the weather event, which is on track to be the most powerful of its kind in recorded history, has been exacerbated by climate change. The “changing weather patterns” referred to by the Kellogg’s spokesperson was an understatement. El Niño is characterized by warming ocean surface temperatures in the Pacific. This kicks off a chain reaction that causes formidable storms and rainfall in the Americas while severely disrupting rainfall patterns and bringing devastating droughts across Asia. Asian rice prices have climbed to their highest in almost two years as El Niño has impacted rice production, Bloomberg reported, while the US Department of Agriculture is forecasting a decline in global rice production for the first time in over a decade. In the grand scheme of things, a cereal reformulation sounds like a trivial casualty in the face of an unfolding climate crisis. But it’s just one of many more to come. More on climate change: World Braces for Nearly a Half Million El Niño Death

Uber on Monday said it is launching a new service that lets customers reserve and pay for parking in major U.S. cities, about nine months after the ride-hailing company acquired parking app SpotHero. The acquisition, announced back in February, gives Uber access to a network of 13,000 garages, lots and valet services across more than 400 cities, the company said. And while Uber’s initial plans will focus on human drivers, the company also plans to leverage these parking spots to help the dozens of autonomous vehicle companies it has partnered with. Uber said the parking spots could be used as staging and waiting locations for autonomous vehicles to help reduce congestion and deadheading, which refers to the miles a vehicle travels without a paying passenger or cargo on board. For now, the company plans to focus on commuters driving their own vehicles. Uber said it will initially launch in 15 U.S. cities, including Atlanta, Austin, Boston, Chicago, Dallas, Denver, Los Angeles, Miami, Nashville, New York City, Philadelphia, San Diego, San Francisco, Seattle and Washington, DC. Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi indicated earlier this month that the company wants the app to be more relevant to commuters. “Commute is one of our largest categories,” he said during Uber’s third-quarter earnings call. “And to be able to offer commuters an Uber to work, a pass to avoid surge, and parking if they want it when they want to drive is a great example of us building out our inventory of services to the commuter who is a top customer for us.” The parking service is the latest in a series of efforts at Uber to build an ecosystem of products that keep customers engaged with its app for everything from hailing a ride and ordering food and groceries, to packing delivery, renting cars, booking hotels, and connecting users with public transit and robotaxis. Uber’s membership program, called Uber One, is a key part of that strategy. Uber One attracts subscribers by offering rewards and credits, perks that have helped the program grow to more than 55 million members as of September 30. For instance, Uber is offering members a chance to earn 10% back in credits on eligible parking reservations. Those credits can later be used for rides and Uber Eats orders. Uber Parking can be accessed directly on the app’s homescreen. Users can enter their destination, select the date and time they need parking, and then review options on an interactive map. Users can filter the results to find self-parking, valet, garages, or surface lots. All pricing is shown upfront. Once a user has found a parking spot, they can book after entering details about their vehicle, and use the payment method in their Uber wallet. Once they arrive at the parking spot, users will either scan a unique QR code or gain entry through license plate recognition. Uber said users will be able to cancel their bookings for free until the start time. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Anthropic joins AI’s natural gas frenzy, buys $3.4B worth of turbines

TechCrunch | 2026-11-09

AI’s power crunch has become so acute that Anthropic, a company that has generally avoided buying physical infrastructure, is spending $3.4 billion on natural gas turbines. The AI company, which is planning to go public this year, is buying Caterpillar generators that equipment supplier OnePWR had originally ordered, according to a Financial Times report. The generators are capable of producing up to 1.7 gigawatts of electricity. Historically, Anthropic has preferred to rent rather than buy. The company disclosed in its S-1 filing in May that it is paying SpaceX subsidiary xAI $1.25 billion per month through May 2029 to use xAI’s computing power. “We expect to enter into additional similar services contracts,” Anthropic stated in the filing, meaning more deals to rent computing power. But as demand for compute ramps up, the market for power has also grown increasingly uncertain, which may have forced its hand. The backlog for natural gas turbines stretches into the early 2030s, and queues to connect to the electrical grid can be just as long. Anthropic’s purchase looks like a last resort. According to the report, OnePWR ordered the turbines a couple years ago, but couldn’t find financing to complete the purchase. Anthropic had planned to buy them from OnePWR but instead went directly to Caterpillar, the manufacturer. It’s not clear why OnePWR was unable to secure financing despite Anthropic’s apparent interest as a customer. Whatever the case, Anthropic likely wanted to keep the generating capacity to itself rather than give a competitor an opportunity to snap it up. AI companies have been ramping up their purchases of natural gas equipment and supplies in recent years. Google, Amazon, Microsoft, and Meta have all announced plans to build massive natural gas power plants to power their data centers on site, rather than drawing from the public grid. The binge has given a second wind to fracking, the drilling method used to extract natural gas from underground rock. And if the buildout proceeds as planned, U.S. data centers alone could consume more natural gas than Germany and Japan combined by 2035. The reliance on natural gas also exposes hyperscalers to price fluctuations. One analyst expects that prices could more than triple by the end of the decade in some markets. If that happens, Anthropic might have a serious case of buyer’s remorse. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Medium’s new TK app brings AI assistance directly into the writing process
2026-11-09

Medium’s new TK app brings AI assistance directly into the writing process

TechCrunch | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Online publishing platform Medium on Monday launched a new app, called TK, which aims to bring AI technology deeper into the writing process. The idea behind TK is to make it easier to turn ideas into written words by incorporating an AI assistant into the editor itself, allowing users to ask for help by typing “@TK” followed by their request or prompt. In publishing and journalism, the term “TK” serves as a placeholder used to mark where missing information can be added later. The “TK” stands for “to come,” but uses a “K” instead of “C” as there are few English words where the “tk” letter combination exists, making it stand out more easily in written text where it could be caught by proofreaders. In Medium’s app, TK takes on a new role as it tries to help users fill in the blanks. The company suggests the AI assistant could help answer questions that you might normally have to switch to another tab to quickly look up, like how many days are in a particular month. The app could also pull in bits of writing you had previously saved to the app when you were still fleshing out your ideas. In addition, Medium says TK will ship with particular skills for editorial feedback, headline feedback, and others that could match your draft to the style guide for Medium publications. Medium’s move to embrace AI in the early stages of writing could prove controversial at a time when the publishing and literary worlds have been reckoning with the role that artificial intelligence has played in the creation of new long-form prose. Multiple authors have lost book deals over suspicions that they used AI technology to help them write, while other literary awards have come under scrutiny after winners were suspected of writing with AI assistance. While writers have long used automated tools for proofreading and light grammatical edits, there is growing concern across the publishing industry that an AI assistant’s editorial “suggestions” can become the writing itself, instead of being reflective of the thoughts in the writer’s own mind. But because AI models were trained on human writing, there are also questions around the reliability of AI detection tools in terms of their ability to properly determine when something was written with AI, rather than with its assistance, versus not at all. TK leans into this debate, taking the side that these nuances will ultimately no longer matter in the end. Instead, the company celebrates what AI can bring to the world of writing, saying that “artificial intelligence makes writing more important, not less,” and proclaiming that “AI is a technology that has the power to amplify human intelligence and it uses the written word as a foundation.” Still, Medium concedes that AI does raise the possibility of low-quality content and blurring the line between what’s human-written versus AI-written. To address these concerns, TK allows authors to publish notes that transparently explain how AI was used in their writing. “This is a start of what we believe is required to publish in the AI era, which is to make the provenance of writing clear, transparent, and inspectable, right down to the prompting and source material,” Medium explained in a blog post about the launch. In addition to writing assistance, TK will help users remember and organize what they had previously written and suggest writing prompts. The app can also optionally connect to your own AI agent through TK’s MCP server or Claude skill, so the app can better understand your projects, your positions, and other ideas that could be stored in your agent’s memory. Meanwhile, the new app incorporates human feedback in a social layer, the company says, allowing writers to share their early drafts with others and get feedback. At launch, TK is available on the web, but a desktop app is on the roadmap, Medium says. The app can import and export Markdown, but it’s not a Markdown app itself. The writing in TK is also not encrypted, as the app is designed for collaboration, but users will be able to set access control permissions on their work. Medium, which is profitable, says TK will be a free tool for those with a Medium account. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. TechCrunch | 2026-11-09

Meta announced on Monday that it’s expanding its AI-powered age-estimation technology on Instagram worldwide to identify more teens and place them under its Teen Account protections, as it faces numerous lawsuits accusing its platforms of harming teens. The social media giant says it will use the AI technology to find people it suspects to be teens, even if they use an adult birthday. Teen Accounts place users into a stricter account experience with additional safeguards, such as receiving direct messages only from people they follow or are already connected to, hiding harmful comments, and setting accounts to private by default. Meta’s age-guessing tools use AI to analyze entire profiles for contextual clues, such as birthday celebrations or mentions of school grades. Meta also looks for these signals across different formats, such as posts, comments, bios, captions, and more. The company also looks at visual cues from photos and videos, including a person’s height or bone structure. Meta said it’s continuing to roll out the technology on Facebook, with a similar global rollout planned for Threads later this year. The global expansion comes as Meta reached settlements worth up to $18 billion with nearly every U.S. state and territory in August to resolve lawsuits alleging its platforms harm teens. As part of the settlements, Meta agreed to improve how it identifies teens and children under 13. “We’ll continue to do our part to proactively ensure teens are in age-appropriate experiences on our apps,” Meta wrote in a blog post. “That’s why as part of our agreement with 52 bipartisan attorneys general across US states, territories, and the District of Columbia, we’re investing in even stronger technology to proactively catch accounts that may belong to children under 13. We’re also strengthening the technology we use to identify accounts that may belong to teens between the ages of 13 and 17 so we can ensure those accounts are placed in experiences designed for teens, even if they give us an adult birthday.” Meta said parents in the U.S. will start seeing an in-feed video on Instagram explaining its approach to ensuring their teens are in age-appropriate protections. Waymo robotaxis kept illegally passing school buses after software fix, feds say

Nearly a year ago, Waymo updated its self-driving software and issued a recall after numerous reports that its robotaxis were illegally passing school buses that had stopped and deployed flashing red lights. Now, federal safety regulators have opened a new investigation into why that behavior has continued. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) said in a November 6 filing that a majority of these reported incidents occurred after the company deployed a software update intended to fix the problem. Reuters was the first to report the filing. Waymo said last year that a software update was completed for all affected vehicles on November 17, 2025. However, NHTSA said the majority of the incidents occurred after the software update. NHTSA’s Office of Defects Investigation (ODI) has opened a “recall query” to “assess the adequacy of the remedy.” The ODI opened its initial investigation into Waymo in October 2025 after seeing footage of one of its autonomous vehicles maneuvering around a stopped school bus that had its stop sign extended, lights flashing, and was unloading children in Atlanta. In that incident, a Waymo robotaxi crossed perpendicularly in front of the school bus from its right side. The autonomous vehicle then turned left around the front of the bus before traveling down the street. Numerous other incidents have been documented in the weeks and months since in Austin, where Waymo also operates a robotaxi service. Austin Independent School District officials reported at least five such incidents at the time, according to a letter filed with NHTSA. Reached for comment, Waymo stressed the importance of safety to its mission. “We have made significant improvements to vehicle behavior around school buses and have received no citations since March 2026,” a spokesperson told TechCrunch via email. “The Waymo Driver has a clearly demonstrated positive impact on roadway safety. Once received, we’ll work to respond to NHTSA’s query and look forward to continuing our engagement on recall processes for AV software that continues to learn and improve.” TechCrunch also reached out to Austin Independent School District officials for comment and will update the article if they respond. Meet Drops, the new music app bringing back the fun of sharing songs with friends
2026-11-09

Meet Drops, the new music app bringing back the fun of sharing songs with friends

TechCrunch | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. There’s a new music discovery app borrowing the endless vertical-scrolling format of TikTok. But instead of watching videos, users swipe through short clips of songs, hoping to stumble upon their next favorite artist. Launched Monday by Harman, a Samsung subsidiary, Drops aims to make discovering new music feel social again. Available on iOS and Android in the U.S., the app lets users scroll through 10- to 30-second snippets of songs, accompanied by lyrics, album artwork, artist photos, and captions. The idea is to give listeners just enough of a track to grab their attention, whether it’s a particularly good lyric, a catchy hook, or a beat that makes them want to hear more. The company says Drops is meant to feel like a return to how people used to discover music in the first place. Before every platform’s algorithm recommendations, people often heard about new songs through friends, music blogs, or online communities. Drops is trying to recreate that experience. “You send a friend a song and say, ‘You have to hear this.’ Then you end up texting, ‘Skip to 1:47, that’s the best part.’ The part that hits usually isn’t the first few seconds, and there wasn’t a good way to share just that moment,” Clare Hunter, general manager of Drops, told us. Hunter explained that the idea for Drops began taking shape after Harman acquired music management software company Roon in 2023. “I used to spend hours making mixtapes for my friends, picking the songs, the order, and decorating the cover. Drops is the version of that I always wanted,” Hunter added. The app is easy to navigate, with tabs including “For You,” “New,” “Showcase,” and “Following,” along with relevant and trending hashtags. Users can like, comment, repost, share, and save tracks to collections called Droplists. They can also send songs directly to other users, follow other profiles, and exchange direct messages. To listen to the entire track, Drops offers integrations with major streaming services, including Apple Music, Qobuz, and TIDAL. Users can jump directly into the full version of the song through their connected streaming platform. Notably, the company says it doesn’t use generative AI for audio or visuals. Songs appear as they were originally released, using official album artwork and licensed artist photography. At a time when AI-generated music and slop are becoming harder to avoid, this will likely appeal to many users. Another part worth noting is that Drops pays rights holders and reports plays back to its partners. Those partners include Universal Music Group, Beggars Group, Empire, Symphonic Distribution, and UnitedMasters. Plays also count toward streams on those services, meaning Drops isn’t trying to replace existing music subscriptions. Drops is currently invite-only, with U.S. users able to join the waitlist. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump questioned the integrity of the midterm elections on Monday, less than a week after the Republican Party suffered historic defeats in both swing states and former GOP strongholds. “Does anybody really believe that this ‘Election’ was honest,” Trump asked in a Truth Social post early Monday. “How could the Polls be so wrong???” “Some early voting ‘dumps’ were so large that it was impossible to win, no matter how many people went to vote,” he continued, before claiming without elaboration: “It’s all about the machines, which are far more important than the voter!” Read more: Welcome to Trump’s Lame-Duck Era Trump’s unsubstantiated fraud claims come nearly two weeks after he told reporters on Oct. 27 that he would respect the election results “if they’re honest elections.” As results came in throughout Nov. 3 and into Nov. 4, Trump seemed to accept the outcome. “My message is, congratulations to the Democrats, and I look forward to working with you for the good of our country,” Trump told CBS News in an election-night phone call. It was a sentiment he repeated in an interview that aired Sunday on 60 Minutes, when he said he would be willing to work with Democrats on immigration, addressing an issue that had been top-of-mind for voters ahead of Election Day. “I think that's something that's actually doable," he said. By Monday morning, however, Trump was casting doubt on the results. The comment harkens back to Trump’s unsubstantiated claims of election interference and ensuing lawsuits after he lost the presidency to Joe Biden in 2020. Trump was sowing doubt on the 2020 election before voting began, telling the public that August that “the only way we’re going to lose this election is if the election is rigged.” Following his loss, Trump refused to concede to Biden. Instead, he began to spread conspiracy theories about widespread election fraud, without evidence. On Nov. 7, 2020, shortly before news organizations projected Biden’s victory, Trump falsely declared on Twitter that he had “WON THIS ELECTION, BY A LOT!” Echoing Trump’s false claims also became a test of loyalty within the Republican Party, with House Speaker Mike Johnson, Ohio Rep. Jim Jordan, and former Georgia Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene among those who espoused the false view. Trump and his allies filed 62 lawsuits challenging or seeking to overturn the results between Nov. 4, 2020, and Jan. 6, 2021, according to the House Jan. 6 committee. Only one produced a victory—a narrow Pennsylvania ruling that didn’t establish fraud or change the outcome. Some cases were dismissed on procedural grounds; others failed on the merits. The legal defeats did not stop Trump from repeating his false claims. On Dec. 22, 2020, he alleged on social media that Democrats had “DUMPED HUNDREDS OF THOUSANDS OF BALLOTS IN THE SWING STATES LATE IN THE EVENING.” The claims escalated on Jan. 5, 2021, when Trump tweeted: “Washington is being inundated with people who don’t want to see an election victory stolen by emboldened Radical Left Democrats.” This followed repeated calls for his supporters to descend upon Washington D.C. and protest the results. As Congress met to count and certify the Electoral College votes on Jan. 6, 2021, a mob of Trump supporters stormed the U.S. Capitol. His efforts to overturn his 2020 defeat also led to criminal charges. In August 2023, Special Counsel Jack Smith brought four federal charges against Trump, alleging efforts to subvert the results through pressure on officials, fraudulent elector slates, and interference with certification. Trump pleaded not guilty. On the campaign trail in 2024, he doubled down on his allegations of election interference by urging voters to deliver a victory that was “too big to rig.” The case was dismissed in November that year, after his reelection, because of Justice Department policy against prosecuting a sitting President.

Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump doubled down on his support of Pete Hegseth amid a Republican-led effort to impeach the embattled Defense Secretary, as Congress returns Monday for a lame-duck session after the Democrats' sweeping victories in the midterms. “He’s guilty of two things: basically winning two wars,” Trump said of Hegseth Sunday evening, praising U.S. military operations in Venezuela and Iran. “It’s awfully hard to say, ‘Let’s impeach somebody who did that.’” The President’s remarks came after he said he still has confidence in Hegseth and referred to efforts of impeachment as "very unfair" during a sit-down interview with CBS' 60 Minutes. Despite Trump’s continued defense of Hegseth, attempts to impeach the Defense Secretary come from within his own party, as Republican Rep. Thomas Massie of Kentucky is preparing to reintroduce a privileged resolution. The measure includes eight articles of impeachment, accusing Hegseth of violating the War Powers Resolution of 1973, citing the lack of congressional oversight on the Iran war which Massie argues was “unlawful from its inception.” Another charge accuses Hegseth of “ignoring laws that minimize civilian casualties.” Outside of the Iran conflict, the charges also allege the “kidnapping of a sovereign foreign leader,” in reference to the U.S.’ capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro and his wife, Cilia Flores, in January—an act Massie says was carried out “without constitutional or legal authority.” Massie's renewed push follows an earlier effort in September, when he sought to force a House vote on impeaching Hegseth. However, Speaker Mike Johnson recessed the House early, preventing the measure from reaching the floor. On Monday, Massie said he's seen some members of the House say Hegseth “has been effective and they agree with the war, so they’ll vote not to impeach,” without naming any lawmakers in particular. “That’s not the question though. The question is whether he committed impeachable acts, and the answer is yes,” Massie argued. If Massie re-files his articles of impeachment against Hegseth as privileged, it would mean ​the House must take up the issue within two legislative days. It’s unclear how this would affect the five-week lame-duck session, as government funding runs out after Dec. 11 and lawmakers still need to make a deal to avert a government shutdown. Hegseth has staunchly pushed back against the impeachment threat. In a video posted to social media, he accused his critics of pursuing a politically-motivated campaign against him. “A disgruntled, defeated Congressman, along with all his Democrat allies, has also dropped sham articles of impeachment against yours truly, just like they tried against President Trump twice,” Hegseth said, referring to the two impeachments of Trump during his first term as President. “It’s all about the politics of personal destruction—didn’t work against President Trump, and it won’t work with me,” he vowed. In a statement to TIME Monday morning, chief Pentagon spokesperson Sean Parnell said Hegseth “will not back down or be deterred by politicians seeking to score partisan points or launch phony investigations where no wrongdoing exists.” He said that the Defense Department has “followed the law and our Secretary will continue to stand and fight every single day to support and defend the Constitution, lead our troops, and protect America” and added that Hegseth is “100% committed to leading the Department until the end of President Trump’s term in office.” TIME has also reached out to the White House for comment. Massie, who lost the Republican primary in May to a challenger endorsed by Trump, already has the support of a number of Democrats, including Hakeem Jeffries, who is widely expected to be the next House Speaker come January. During an appearance on ABC's This Week Sunday, Jeffries said: “I’m a hard 'yes' on the Hegseth resolution.” Accusing Hegseth of abusing his position, Jeffries continued. “The so-called Secretary of Defense has abused his power, undermined our national security and engineered this reckless and costly war of choice in Iran, which has skyrocketed prices on the American people and made the country less safe.” Jeffries follows in the footsteps of Rep. Adam Smith, the top Democrat in the House Armed Services Committee, and Rep. Ro Khanna of California, who have also pledged to back Massie’s impeachment efforts. In the fallout of the midterm rout, the Republican Party appears to be split over the Iran war—and the extent of its impact on voters. Vice President J.D. Vance said the day after the election that “the reason we lost is the war.” Trump, in his 60 Minutes interview that was filmed Friday and released Sunday, said he didn't disagree with Vance's analysis, but doubled down on the inception of the war. “The only thing I wish he would have said there, if he felt it, and you'd have to ask him about that, but, ‘If you had it to do again, would you do it?’” Trump said, before adding: “My answer is an emphatic 'yes.'” The Iran war remains vastly unpopular with Americans. A midterm exit poll showed that 74% of voters thought the Iran war was a crucial or major factor when casting their ballot. The conflict is estimated to have cost American taxpayers around $47.1 billion so far. The financial fallout of the war has added to the cost-of-living pressures facing many Americans, with soaring diesel prices proving to be a key concern among voters. Democrats, meanwhile, have long argued for more congressional overview regarding the Iran war. Some Republicans, including Massie and Alaska Sen. Lisa Murkowski, have also questioned the boundaries of the presidential war powers. In a Senate Appropriations Committee hearing in July, Murkowski questioned Hegseth as to why Congress hadn’t been asked for authorization. Hegseth, in response, acknowledged the “long-standing legal debate on this particular topic” but insisted that the Department of Defense shares “the position of the White House, that we have all the authorities necessary at this moment.”
