Update your preferences in Account Settings Sama's former office in Nairobi, pictured in 2022. The company traded under the name Samasource Impact Sourcing, Inc. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The embattled AI data annotation firm Sama is winding down its operations and liquidating its assets, according to a letter to creditors reviewed by TIME. The letter, dated Nov. 6, blames “declining revenues due to increased competition that was compounded by recent class action litigation that would have required significant resources and years to defend successfully.” The letter does not specify which class-action litigation contributed to Sama's financial difficulties. The company has faced separate such lawsuits in Kenya and the U.S. An unaudited balance sheet attached to the letter shows Sama had approximately $1 million in assets and $17 million in liabilities as of October. TIME previously reported that Sama performed content moderation for Meta and data labeling for OpenAI, work that employees said exposed them to traumatic content without adequate safeguards. Read More: Inside Facebook’s African Sweatshop Sama and Meta are facing lawsuits in Kenya related to allegations that former Facebook content moderators were exploited and illegally terminated after they raised concerns, and that Sama illegally suppressed a unionization effort. Specifically, Daniel Motaung, a former Sama moderator who was fired in 2019 after trying to organize a strike, sued Sama and Meta in 2022, claiming violations of constitutional rights including freedom of association, dignity and fair working conditions, as well as alleged forced labour and human trafficking. His petition has since been consolidated with a separate one brought by former moderators who allege they were unlawfully made redundant and blacklisted. Sama has said it complied with Kenyan labor law, and Meta denied responsibility for the working conditions. Earlier this year, Sama separately came under fire for its work handling data from Meta’s AI-powered smart glasses. Workers told Swedish newspapers Svenska Dagbladet and Göteborgs-Posten that they were required to review private content from the glasses’ cameras, including people using the toilet, changing clothes, and having sex. Sama was initially named as a defendant in several proposed class-action privacy lawsuits against Meta and glasses manufacturer Luxottica, but plaintiffs voluntarily dropped their claims against Sama in August. (Meta denies the claims and says it will fight them, adding that while it may review data from smart glasses to improve its products, it takes steps to filter out private information. Luxottica did not respond to a request for comment.) On October 23, Samasource Impact Sourcing, Inc., the U.S. company operating as Sama,  transferred its assets to a newly formed company, Sama ABC, to oversee their sale and distribute the proceeds to creditors. Sama ABC is administered by “professionals who specialize in liquidating insolvent or otherwise financially distressed companies for the benefit of their creditors,” according to the letter. Despite the similar names, the letter says Sama ABC is not corporately affiliated with the company. “Sama leaves a highly regarded legacy as being one of the earliest champions of impact sourcing: a model centered on deliberately hiring individuals from marginalized communities in East Africa,” the letter states. “Through this model, the company directly impacted over seventy thousand lives with life skills and technical training and employment in digital work at high living wages and benefits. Its impact extended throughout the communities it served due to the material wealth ripple effect created by its targeted employment model.” The letter notes that a bank has a priority claim to “substantially all” of the company's assets. The letter does not say what the liquidation means for the ongoing case in Kenya. An email to Sama’s press inbox bounced. Sama’s CEO, Wendy Gonzalez, did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Sama ABC representatives did not respond to emails or phone calls.

Introducing TIME100 Climate: The 100 Most Influential Leaders Driving Business Climate Action
2026-11-09

Introducing TIME100 Climate: The 100 Most Influential Leaders Driving Business Climate Action

TIME | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sometimes you need to climb 30 ft. in the air, atop a modified oil rig, to get a better view of what’s coming next. In October, TIME’s Justin Worland did just that when he traveled to southwestern Utah, where Fervo Energy is adopting fossil-fuel technologies for a rapidly changing energy landscape. When we launched TIME100 Climate in 2023, funding from the Biden-era Inflation Reduction Act, the single largest climate investment in American history, was starting to flow through the U.S. economy, making the country an exemplar of how government policy can influence climate action. Three years later, we’re in a very different world—with signals that new investment is being driven less by direction from Washington than by desire from markets. In May, Fervo Energy staged one of the largest U.S. clean-energy initial public offerings in history. This was fueled by the geothermal-energy company’s Cape Station plant in Utah, with 100 MW of power scheduled by the end of 2026 and an additional 400 MW planned in 2028—which would be enough to power 500,000 homes. In September, Google signed a landmark deal for the plant to supply power to a future data center in the area. That promise of clean data-center power is largely driving the enthusiasm of this new moment. Investments in clean energy will nearly double those in fossil fuels this year, according to the International Energy Agency. “Around the world, countries and companies are turning to technologies that were developed as climate solutions and now also offer energy security, speed, reliability, and increasingly attractive economics,” Worland writes in this issue. Our 2026 TIME100 Climate, co-edited by Emma Barker Bonomo and Kyla Mandel, looks at Fervo’s CEO Tim Latimer and 99 other individuals who are leading and navigating this transformation. To assemble this group, our editors and correspondents reviewed hundreds of names, with a particular focus on those who achieved measurable and sustainable action over the past 12 months. What we found was a global story: At Start Campus in Portugal, CEO Robert Dunn led the design and development of the 1.2-gigawatt Sines Data Campus, an AI data center to be run entirely on renewable power and cooled by seawater. At the end of last year, Microsoft announced a $10 billion investment in Sines. In neighboring Spain, CEO Mario Ruiz-Tagle has led Iberdrola España through a period of rapid expansion of the country’s wind and solar resources, which set the average Spanish household up to see a 10-euro savings on monthly energy bills since March, even as Europe is hit hard by high oil prices. It’s noteworthy that value—not values—is driving much of this change. While advocates have argued for decades that shifting to sustainable, renewable, noncarbon energy is simply the right thing to do, people everywhere are finding that moving away from fossil fuels is the right move for their bottom lines. India’s Mahindra Last Mile Mobility, led by CEO Suman Mishra, grew 85% year-over-year in the first quarter of fiscal year 2027 to become the country’s market leader in electric three-wheelers. In Chile, Juan Carlos Muñoz spearheaded Chile’s transition to electric public transportation; Santiago now has the largest fleet outside of China. And in Kenya and Rwanda, BasiGo CEO Jit Bhattacharya is pioneering leased electric buses and charging infrastructure to cut diesel buses. These innovators join a host of groundbreaking thinkers, investors, and pioneers who continue to write new playbooks for climate action, reminding us the world is full of leaders keeping their eyes on the future.

The license can be legally worked around: 1. Have one AI agent document the code fully by explaining how it works in plain english2. Copy that new documentation to an entirely segregated second agent3. Instruct the new agent to write brand new code according to the documentation It's unlikely this will be done for all OSS projects since the result usually has bugs and inconsistencies. But spend a week or two doing passes over it for improvement and you get a functional reimplementation. (What license that can use will depend on how the law around AI code shakes out in the future) I would say as soon as even one line of the original code was in the training data, this becomes a copyright violation. "Clean room" means nothing of the inner workings of the original besides the spec ever made it to the implementation team. And you have to be able to prove that. First off, you can always just use a LLM with an open training dataset (OLMo, Pythia, etc) to prove that the data isn't in it, and have that handle the "clean" side of the equation. Secondly, you are under the mistaken impression that a LLM that has been exposed (which, BTW, does not mean "memorized", or even any rough understandings of the workings - ask the LLM (ideally an abliterated one to avoid refusals) to reproduce the original code verbatim, it'll almost certainly fail badly) automatically fails in such a suit. It does not. There is no such thing as "homeopathic copyright contamination". Copyvio requires substantial similarity. LLMs will tend to have common algos memorized, but those are the very things that tend to be simultaneously not copyrightable. The cleanroom is an evidentiary shield, not a pillar of copyright law. If you can prove that the cleanroom team had no access to the original, then you can prove that they didn't copy it, and potentially get summary judgement. If you can't prove that, that doesn't mean that it's not copyvio, it just means that you instead have to focus on substantial-similarity defenses. Access without substantial similiarity is not copyvio. If you read an open-source codebase, close it, and then later write a new program purely on your own that looks nothing like the original, you had "access", but still did not infringe. The same applies to LLMs - but even moreso, because humans are much better at learning from brief, limited exposures to content than LLMs are. A court will usually apply an "Abstraction-Filtration-Comparison" test - they break the code down into layers (source code, parameters, architecture, algos, etc) (abstraction), remove everything that copyright law does not protect (ideas and common algos, things where there's only a limited number of ways to possibly do it, etc) (filtration), and then compare the creative expression that remains to see if there exists sufficient similarity to count as infringement. It is highly unlikely that a LLM-implemented version will fail that. (That said, you're talking about a long, expensive legal battle if you get to that point, so you obviously prefer to be able to get summary judgement if you can) Oh, also, as pointed out further downthread [slashdot.org], any LLM with a training cutoff before the license change date is pretty much assured to be okay for use in a cleanroom implementation. Copyright holders cannot make retroactive copyright changes, and open source licenses generally explicitly grant rights in perpetuity anyway. The threat would be new code. But since the cleanroom side has never seen that new code (it was released after the training date), it remains untainted. For civil law it tends to depend on the mood of the judge on the day, unfortunately, but is in theory whoever can exceed 50% probability will prevail. Mostly hybrids (28%), but also diesel (4%). The average cost of electricity in the UK is 26p/kwh. A typical EV can go 6 km per kWh, so that's 4.3p/km. The average cost of a liter of petrol ("gasoline") is 170p. The average car in the UK can go 13 km on a liter. 170/13 = 13p/km. So driving a petrol car costs three times as much as driving an EV. An EV owner can sign up for cheaper nighttime rates at half the daytime rate, which would make driving a petrol car six times as expensive as driving an EV. Elizabeth Smart Has Taken Perhaps the Last Job You’d Ever Expect
2026-11-08

Elizabeth Smart Has Taken Perhaps the Last Job You’d Ever Expect

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Elizabeth Smart has been telling her story for over a decade, in memoir, onstage, and recently, in a Netflix documentary. She doesn’t flinch from its horrific details: how the captors who kidnapped her in 2002—one who believed he was Mormon prophet and his wife—held her in the woods, where she was routinely tied up, starved, and sexually assaulted. This year, Smart took perhaps the last job you’d ever expect: true-crime podcast host. In The Survivor Files, she talks to other survivors of kidnapping and sexual violence, and they really dig into the details. Her show has all the trappings of true-crime podcasts you’ve heard before—911 tape-recordings, foreboding music—but here’s the difference: The women at the center of these crimes are telling us their experiences. We’re not just consuming their stories. We’re bearing witness, and hearing from them what came after. On a recent episode of Death, Sex & Money, host Anna Sale spoke to Smart about helping survivors take command of their own stories. This transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. Anna Sale: On The Survivor Files, you talk to survivors whose stories have a lot in common with yours, and ones who experienced totally different crimes. Elizabeth Smart: Not everyone is going to feel like they have a connection to me, but I find it hard to believe that if you are a survivor, you wouldn’t find that with the other survivors I have on my new podcast. Someone is going to speak to you and you’re going to be like, I know how that feels. OK, I feel inspired to keep going. When someone shares that they’ve been raped with me, I’m not going turn to them and be like, “Well, what were you wearing? Why didn’t you scream? Why didn’t you report?” Hopefully it helps change all of that. I want to ask you as a peer, as an interviewer, what your experience has been in that role on the show. You said in the New York Times that you wanted to help your guests walk through, beat by beat, what happened—because if you don’t understand the depth of how dark it was, you said, how can you understand how incredibly bright they shine now? When you’re interviewing someone who has gone through a terrible trauma, how have you thought about asking them to retell it for a public audience, having been in that position over and over again yourself? When I sit down and I interview someone, I do try to think of myself, of how I’ve felt when people have asked me questions that I didn’t like or that I felt like could’ve been asked better. I try to approach the stories from that angle: How would I feel if I was asked this question? Is there a better way I could ask it, a way that would put them more at ease? I want this to be an empowering experience for them. I want them to feel pride in sharing their story, I don’t want it to be something that’s dragging them backwards. I want to ask you about retelling your story for the Netflix documentary. You are on camera and you are retelling the story of what happened. You retell it in a similar tone to how we’re talking now. I never worried for you in the way that you talked. I was struck that for the other people in your family, it seemed much more difficult for them to go back to that time on camera. Did you notice that too? A lot of them don’t talk about it. It was the hardest part of their life, and it was the worst time in their life. Unless it’s serving a purpose, who wants to continually live in the worst part of their life? So I mean, my sister, you know, she doesn’t go back down that path every day. She’s a schoolteacher, she works with special ed students every day. Everyone’s moved on with their life. I have moved on with my life, but in a direction where I feel like my story can serve a greater purpose. There’s a reason for it to be in my life still, whereas for the rest of my family, their jobs do not encompass the past. Their day-to-day lives do not involve my abduction. Regarding that greater purpose, what have you noticed has happened because you have been able to talk clearly and unblinkingly about what happened to you and about how your life looks now? What do you hope that it’s changing in the culture or changing for people who’ve been the victim of trauma or sexual violence? I wouldn’t say it’s just me. There have been people working in this field for many years longer than I’ve even been alive. But I just hope that by me sharing my story, I’m making the world a safer place for victims to come forward and disclose. There’s so much crossover between my experience and so many other people’s experiences. For example, my experience of kidnapping and abduction versus a survivor of domestic abuse. For years I was asked, “Why didn’t you run? Why didn’t you speak out?” And then I hear people say to domestic abuse victims, “You had a car, why didn’t you just drive away? You had a debit card, why didn’t you just do something?” I’ve heard them get asked those kinds of questions. And then for me, I can say, “I have experienced both being physically chained up but then also had so much fear and threats made against my life that they created mental chains around me.” With physical chains, you’re like, OK, I can see how I could get out of this. If I could reach the bolt cutters, I could cut through this. But those mental chains, they are invisible. Usually they’re built upon years and years of manipulation and threat and abuse. It’s not straightforward. So by me just being able to talk about that and talk about how my captors were able to manipulate me, how they were able to control me, then I’ve had survivors from this other world of domestic abuse be like, You know, I wasn’t kidnapped, but that’s exactly how I felt in my abusive relationship. From the outside it maybe looked like I could have just gotten in the car and driven away, but it wasn’t that clear-cut. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. There’s No Road Map to Democrats’ Success. But There’s a Direct Route to Their Failure.
2026-11-07

There’s No Road Map to Democrats’ Success. But There’s a Direct Route to Their Failure.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter that totally predicted Democrats would get 260 House seats and 56 Senate seats in our heads weeks ago but, you know, didn’t feel like telling anyone. Well that was sump’m else, huh? Crazy to think that Democrats were toast two years ago and that, starting in January, every American will be required to pledge daily allegiance to a nonbinary god. But anyway, the 2026 midterms are old news—what’s going on with the 2028 Republican presidential field? And could Democrats have picked up more than a measly nine Senate seats? Will anyone not vote to impeach Pete Hegseth? Let’s first look ahead to what Democrats are planning to do with these majorities beyond passing national Sharia law. If there’s any downside to flipping (likely) nine Senate seats and winning the largest House majority this century, it’s convincing the voters who brought you there that they’ve already seen the greatest accomplishments of the next two years. The very act of winning ends Republicans’ partisan legislative agenda. It ends Donald Trump’s ability to gift 37-year-old brainmelt cases lifetime appointments to the federal bench. Trump will not be able to get another Supreme Court nominee confirmed come January. He will not be allowed to do as he pleases with the understanding that congressional committees will willingly ignore it. The most important elements of the incoming Democratic agenda, in other words, have already been completed. In terms of how Democrats will spend their next two years in Congress, though, there’s not much to overthink. They were elected principally on two issues: lowering the cost of living and reining in corruption. They should stick to those. On the cost of living, they will vote early and often to end Trump’s tariff regime, either by disapproving existing ones or passing new legislation reclaiming congressional tariff authority. They will vote early and often on war powers resolutions to end the Iran war. They will vote to reverse Medicaid cuts—new work requirements for which will kick in next year—and Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program cuts, which have already kicked in. They’ll vote to restore enhanced Affordable Care Act subsidies to lower health insurance premiums. In short, they’ll vote to reverse all of the MAGA moves that voters hated and that cost Republicans the election, leaving Republicans the choice to filibuster, veto, or sustain Trump’s veto on any of these popular moves. Trickier tactical decisions will come on must-pass government-funding bills. Most notably, Democrats can use the power of the purse to cut off funding for the Iran war, and they’ll attempt something along those lines in their opening bids. But we can already see many of the new, novice Democratic members and senators from red states getting queasy when the question becomes whether to shut down the government over the issue, even though Democrats (and Republicans, for that matter) have heard pretty resoundingly from the voters that they want this war finished. On corruption, there’s more to do beyond oversight hearings—and there will be oh so many of those. Because those hearings can and should fold into legislation. Among the many things we’re eager to learn more about is the level of insider trading, favor granting, and prediction-market gambling from within the White House. We suspect the findings will be substantial. And that’s a great opportunity to extend popular proposed bans on congressional stock trading to the White House, as well as to ban prediction-market betting in the executive branch. As important as any individual policy, though, is the need to stick to them, and to stay focused squarely on what midterm voters demanded without getting sidetracked by factional demands from one segment of these big, broad majorities that put them at odds with the other. Democrats were handed a gift from God in these midterms that they didn’t actually deserve. The Democratic coalition had been in a death spiral, having comprehensively lost the white working-class vote and watching anxiously as minority working-class voters drifted in the same direction. Without some correction, the Democratic coalition would soon be down to English majors. Thanks to Trump doing everything in his power to shatter the very groups that got him elected, Democrats were able to earn a tryout in the Great Plains, in the Deep South, in Texas. If they want to stay a viable party in the Senate and the Electoral College, it’s imperative to show that they’ll stick to the program. Vice President J.D. Vance’s presidential hopes nosedived on Election Day, and he knows it. He’s the deputy to, and chief public cheerleader for, the very same president that the nation comprehensively rejected. And his legacy contribution to the midterm campaigns was to serve as chief public cheerleader for Ohio gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy after the candidate weaponized his opponent’s childhood rape against her. The 2028 presidential nomination has never looked more up for grabs. So it wasn’t surprising that his damage control came quickly and at length in a Twitter essay the following morning. And it was our first look at how he would attempt to balance his day job with his need to separate himself from his day job. “The reason we lost is the war,” Vance wrote. “The president himself said so last night.” OK, sure, Trump admitted that energy prices were the factor. It gets more interesting in the next part. “If you’d like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so,” he added. “Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that’s at least honest.” To whom is this directed? He’s braying vaguely against neocons and hawks within the party. But he’s not allowed to be the avatar of the anti-war faction, as he’d like to be, because his boss started the war and made Vance a spokesperson for it. Maybe Trump will give him permission to become an open opponent of the war—he’s generally pretty loosey-goosey about demanding loyalty from his subjects, right? Right. Best of luck to Vance as he figures all this out! He’s not the only presidential hopeful entering the essay contest. (It’s not a coincidence that these guys all have between one and two Ivy League diplomas.) Ted Cruz, now the lone statewide-elected Republican in the postrevolutionary Woke Texas, rattled off and gave credence to a whole list of voters’ concerns: “Food is too high, gas is too high, diesel is too high, insurance is too high, life is too high,” he wrote. “Tariffs have made everything too expensive, and the Iran war has dragged on too long.” He observes these things as if they just came out of the blue. Why are there tariffs? Why is there an Iran war? This man behind the curtain goes unnamed. The game, then, is correctly blaming Trump’s policies for why the GOP crashed and burned, without ascribing those policy choices to a source. For the 80th time: It’s not complicated why the GOP lost! Trump made things more expensive to amuse himself, while launching a losing war, while enriching himself, while being a jerk, while overplaying his hand on immigration enforcement, while building monuments to himself, while fighting hard to keep the Epstein files hidden, etc., etc. But let’s look at some of the other excuses out there. Many of them come from Trump. He has argued that “Fox News bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition, and worse,” a thought that came to him while watching former New York City Mayor Bill de Blasio on the channel. We’d rank de Blasio’s persuasive abilities on cable news as relatively low on the reasons Republicans lost. Trump’s bigger argument is that Republicans lost because “I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!” It’s true Republicans get much better turnout among low-propensity GOP voters when Trump is on the ballot, or when it’s a presidential election cycle in general. But presidents are never on the ballot in the midterms—which come in the “middle” of a president’s “term”—and their party doesn’t typically lose the House popular vote by 14 points. Former Homeland Security Secretary Kristi Noem, a notable supporting figure in the administration’s collapse, posted: “We were too successful the first year of the Trump administration.” Ah, yes, the winning was all too front-loaded, cannibalizing wins that could have been had closer to election time. But Trump’s approval rating has been a downward line since Day 1. Some right-wing commentators blamed the failure of Senate Republicans to pass the SAVE America Act, the GOP’s strict voter-ID and voter-verification law, for the losses. And, sure, maybe its burdensome requirements could have disenfranchised some new registrants from exercising their right to vote. But can we now finally finish talking about the need for the SAVE America Act? Here are some of the states with the strictest voter-ID requirements and, in parentheses, how the GOP fared in those states: Indiana (yikes), Ohio (yikes), Mississippi (yikes), Georgia (yikes), North Carolina (YIKES). What that also does show, on the other hand, is that Democrats have an opportunity, should they want to ever attempt a bipartisan election-reform bill, to compromise with Republicans on a form of national voter ID without being so • We feel as if we’ve read less articles and seen fewer questions about whether Trump will “moderate” after the midterms or “change” in some way after seeing the nation’s verdict. Everyone simply knows he won’t. And Trump made his intentions especially clear on Friday, when he announced that he was replacing chief of staff Susie Wiles with Stephen Miller, the architect and executor of various policies—especially the immigration-related ones—that led to Tuesday’s wipeout. On the other hand, he’s a pretty accomplished guy: He was able to turn Texas blue, after all. • A unifying position among Democrats across the political spectrum this fall was that they should impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, the erstwhile weekend-cable-news co-host who pushed for and did not win the Iran war while firing and/or alienating all the competent people around him. But Democrats may not get the chance next year, because it may happen next week. When the House reconvenes, Republican Rep. Thomas Massie will force a vote on Hegseth’s impeachment. All, or nearly all, Democrats will support it. But how many Republicans will? We’d expect a lot of interest. A great many House Republicans lost their seats because of this guy and now have to come back for a lame-duck session. Would you be in a “Let’s vote to save Pete Hegseth” kind of mood while you were cleaning out your office after losing an R +15 district? They might as well try to do it by unanimous consent. Hegseth sure seems to be preparing for a trial. • Democrats may be on the verge of flipping nine Senate seats. Let’s chastise them for not getting more! There are two glaring missed opportunities on the map. Republican Julia Letlow won the Louisiana Senate race by only 1.8 points against Democrat Jamie Davis. This race had similar characteristics to Mississippi and South Carolina: stunningly high Black voter turnout after the Supreme Court’s latest whack at the Voting Rights Act, and a ho-hum Republican candidate with no competitive general-election experience. Perhaps Senate Democrats’ super PAC could’ve thrown a couple million bucks its way. And in Montana, the opposition to Republican Kurt Alme was split between Democrat Alani Bankhead and independent Seth Bodnar, when Bodnar should’ve just cut it with the cutesiness and beat Bankhead in the Democratic primary. He probably would have won. Maybe in the next rare historic tsunami with no modern comparison! • There’s always one drop early on election night that tells you where things are going. In 2020, Joe Biden’s underwhelming early vote margin in Miami-Dade County suggested that there was going to be a massive polling error that year. In 2024, it was Kamala Harris’ underperformance in Loudoun County, Virginia. This year, it was the wildly terrible early vote numbers for Victoria Spartz in Indiana’s 5th District. Spartz ended up losing her “likely Republican”–rated seat, while other Indiana Republicans held on. The only reason those other Indiana Republicans may have held on, though, is because state Republicans resisted ferocious pressure from the administration, and Speaker Mike Johnson, to maximally gerrymander their state. Trump punished those reluctant state senators by successfully primarying them out of office. In the end, their sacrifice may have been the only thing that prevented a wipeout of the whole delegation. • Elections aren’t always one-sided disasters, so let’s look at all the Democratic seats that Republicans flipped in the House. They flipped Tennessee’s 9th District, which state Republicans had gerrymandered into a Trump +21 seat, by a single point. They won one (1) of the five Texas districts they attempted to gerrymander Democrats out of: Texas’ 32nd. And last but not least, former Maine Gov. Paul LePage won Maine’s 2nd District. He certainly benefited from the performance of Susan Collins, who, even in losing, was the only Senate candidate to improve upon Republicans’ statewide margin relative to 2024. Anyway, that’s the whole list as of this writing. And LePage’s district, which Trump won by 9 in 2024, appears to be the bluest seat held by a Republican. Tsunamis (electoral) are nuts! • After having bequeathed the lousy Democrats the largest House majority for either party this century, Johnson declared his work complete and announced he wouldn’t stay on as House Republican leader to lead a hollowed-out conference that will have no relevance whatsoever for the next two years. The race to succeed him appears to be between Reps. Jim Jordan and Steve Scalise. Our first thought is: Who gives a shit? Our second thought is: See first thought. In the wake of this week’s sweeping midterm rebuke of Donald Trump, Protect Democracy co-founder Ian Bassin joins Dahlia Lithwick to discuss how voters pushed back against authoritarianism—and how the country can turn that victory into lasting change. Bassin argues that congressional investigators must do more than expose corruption: They should preserve the evidence a future attorney general would need to recoup taxpayers’ money. Did Trump Learn Anything From the Midterms? We Already Have Our Answer.
2026-11-06

Did Trump Learn Anything From the Midterms? We Already Have Our Answer.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. After suffering a historic rebuke in this week’s midterms, the infamously brash Donald Trump sounded almost chastened. “We’ll, you know, look forward to working with them,” he said of Democrats in a subdued election-night phone call with the New York Times. “We’ll probably work something out.” Even as Trump emphasized his power to veto legislation, his postelection comments led some to imagine that a humbled dealmaker in chief might really be willing to play ball. “Donald Trump may be a bit crazy, but he is not stupid, and he is a good politician,” Sen. Bernie Sanders said on MSNBC, suggesting the president might even be open to signing a Medicare for All bill into law. In the end, Trump’s conciliatory tone lasted less than three days. On Friday afternoon, the president announced that he was elevating Stephen Miller, an anti-immigrant hawk who has been with him since the 2016 campaign, to be his next White House chief of staff, describing Miller as “one of my most trusted advisors.” Democrats, including some who previously sounded hopeful about persuading Trump to back legislation that would lower costs for inflation-pinched Americans, reacted with fury. “Stephen Miller is a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands,” wrote Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, the likely next House speaker. “His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today.” It isn’t unusual for presidents to reshuffle the org chart after a midterm drubbing. But those personnel changes are usually a tacit admission that something has gone wrong, a public hitting of the reset button. After Republicans flipped the House in 2010, Barack Obama replaced Rahm Emanuel, his feisty chief of staff, with Bill Daley, a pragmatic centrist whom Obama hoped could help him work across the aisle. In the wake of a brutal loss, letting certain heads roll can look like a politically savvy sop to the will of the voters. But Trump, who infamously tried to overturn his 2020 election defeat, has never cared much for the will of the voters. And promoting Miller, who is among the administration’s most despised figures, is the opposite of what most presidents would do after an overwhelming electoral beatdown. Even among Trump aides, Miller has an unusually long history of bigotry. In high school, he criticized administrators for allowing Spanish-language announcements and once reportedly jumped into a girls’ track race in an apparent attempt to prove the athletic superiority of men. In college, he wrote columns for Duke’s student newspaper that criticized feminism and argued that “Christianity is embedded in the very soul of our nation.” As an aide to an Alabama senator, Miller promoted white-supremacist talking points, including the racist “great replacement” conspiracy theory. After arriving at the White House, he zealously defended separating migrant families at the border. In the president’s second term, Miller helped architect Operation Metro Surge, the January immigration-enforcement blitz in Minneapolis that killed two American citizens whom Miller falsely branded domestic terrorists and assassins. At first glance, doubling down on Miller now looks like a strange choice even for Trump. The president appears to be well aware of how extreme his adviser’s views are. As the Wall Street Journal reported this week in a midterms postmortem, Trump has privately admitted that Miller’s zeal for deportations had “gone too far.” The evidence suggests that backlash to those efforts helped sweep Republicans out of power across the country this week, particularly among Latino voters. But promoting Miller starts to make more sense when you consider who Trump is at his core: a confrontational man who managed to get elected president twice in large part by stoking divisions over immigration. Ever in search of a scapegoat for his own failures, Trump has reportedly blamed Republicans’ hefty midterm losses on Susie Wiles, whom Miller is set to replace as chief of staff. And rather than confront the reality of defeat, Trump appears to be seeking succor among his core supporters. “Prominent MAGA figures I talked to this week cited Stephen Miller as their dream choice for chief of staff,” the Daily Mail reporter Charlie Spiering posted after the news broke. “This pick really re-energizes a gloomy base.” The president also prizes loyalty, and Miller spent the years between Trump’s terms laying the groundwork for a “spectacular migration crackdown.” We’ve also lived through a version of this before. In 2018, after Democrats retook the House, Trump sounded similar notes of conciliation. “We have a lot of things in common on infrastructure,” he said the day after the election. “We want to do something on healthcare, they want to do something on healthcare, there are a lot of great things we could do together.” None of it ended up happening. Instead, the president soon found himself in a standoff with Democrats over his demand that a government funding bill include money for his southern border wall, an impasse that yielded what was then the longest government shutdown in U.S. history. A repeat performance may already be in the offing. The federal government will run out of money next month, and elevating Miller isn’t the only sign that Trump plans to keep immigration at the forefront. “Deportations will continue,” Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin posted after Democrats won the House and Senate. Even Vice President J.D. Vance, who on Wednesday published a 600-word X post blaming the war in Iran for Republicans’ dismal midterm, showed little interest in finding common ground over immigration. “I’m open to the idea that we should change enforcement—by focusing more on businesses that hire illegal labor, for instance,” Vance wrote. “But anyone pushing amnesty is lying to you.” On Friday, Vance put out a statement congratulating Miller on his promotion. Elevating Miller isn’t the only sign that Trump plans to spend the last two years of his second term the same way he spent the first two. Democrats won key races by casting the president as an out-of-touch billionaire more interested in beautifying Washington than in making groceries cheaper, but Trump is already hitting the gas on finishing his White House ballroom and erecting a triumphal arch near the Lincoln Memorial. After an election in which many voters rejected building data centers to power artificial intelligence, Trump reiterated in a Friday post on Truth Social that “only foolish leadership will say ‘NO’ to the riches they bring!” And even as key Trump allies seem to want the president to make Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth the fall guy for Iran, some reporters think replacing Wiles with Miller could save Hegseth’s job. But the core issue of Miller’s promotion is immigration, the subject of the incoming chief of staff’s overriding obsession. For nearly two years now, this administration’s deportation policies have brought death, violence, and misery to communities across the country. With Miller at the helm, it would be a surprise if the next two years turn out much better. On that cheery end-of-week note, let’s pivot to my colleagues’ suggestions about how to get your weekend off to a happier start: Take stock of election week: It has been a truly wild week in politics, one that we’ll be making sense of for months and even years to come. Need some help? Tomorrow, the Surge—Slate’s weekly political analysis newsletter—will send out its postelection edition. We got an early look, and it’s a great one. You’ll want to sign up here. A school staple gets revisited: Millions of students had to read the coming-of-age wilderness survival novel Hatchet in English class. It turns out that there’s a reason the book was inescapable for so many kids. Luke Winkie gets to the bottom of its unexpectedly controversial reputation. A highly anticipated sequel falls flat: Romantasy fans have been waiting for more than five years for the latest installment in Sarah J. Maas’ A Court of Thorns and Roses series. Now that A Court of Splintered Harmony is finally here, it feels frustratingly incomplete. Nadira Goffe explains why some fans feel shortchanged by this sequel. A quintessential American author gets his due: There are countless American writers who have shaped the country’s literary landscape, but one famous figure rarely gets mentioned in the conversation. Laura Miller makes the case that Edgar Allan Poe—whose work deliberately eschews many of the essential themes of American literature—is actually the most influential American writer of them all. A time-traveling sequel arrives: The Time Traveler’s Wife was a blockbuster hit in 2003. Now, more than 20 years later, a sequel has arrived. Ironically, the inventive time-travel tale feels of a totally different era from its predecessor. Rebecca Onion assesses some of the book’s mind-bending challenges for readers. Congratulations on making it to Friday! You’ve earned a break from the news, but I promise we’ll be back in your inbox on Monday with more. Enjoy the weekend. Machine learning researchers build a better world model
2026-11-09

Machine learning researchers build a better world model

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Leibniz lives! Insight about how 'nature makes no leaps' promises better robot predictions Machine learning researchers have developed a technique that allows world models to make better predictions about how objects will behave. World models are used to train physical AI systems like robots how to navigate the real world. They predict the consequences of actions. But they do so based on observed data. These models could provide better direction to robots if they were able to make inferences about possible actions without being trained for all the possible outcomes. Randall Balestriero (Brown University, AMI Labs), Kerda Varaku, and Turing Award winner Yann LeCun (NYU, AMI Labs) describe an approach that might allow that to happen in a pre-print paper, "Counterfactual World Models: Natura Non Facit Saltus." The Latin refers to Gottfried Leibniz's axiom "nature makes no leaps," which had a good run until it ran headlong into quantum mechanics. Yet for the authors, the saying explains how they have been able to devise a method for zero-shot physics inference. Counterfactual world models, said Balestriero in an email to The Register use "the mismatch between your prediction and the real world to figure out what did you get wrong, and from that you can better understand what happened for better future predictions, debugging, reasoning, all at test time." Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) models, developed by LeCunn and colleagues back in 2023, have been proposed for robots because they can take an action as input and ask what the world would be like with that action. But a JEPA world model produces an average of outcomes and would not be able to anticipate counterfactual scenarios – events that didn't happen. The technique that the researchers developed is not specific to JEPAs – it can be applied to any world model that predicts the average next state under any action. But JEPA models are particularly well suited to being adapted. Balestriero said that the counterfactual world model experiments were done using off-the-shelf encoders (DINOv2 and MAE) without any fine-tuning and only a small predictor trained on top. Creating a counterfactual world model from that starting point would only require minor additions. Counterfactual world models predict alternative scenarios more effectively by inferring possibilities from what did occur. "In causal inference, answering 'What would have happened had I acted differently?' takes three steps," explained Balestriero. "You use what you observed to learn something about those hidden factors, you change the action, and you predict again with the hidden factors kept exactly as they were. "Our counterfactual world model does all three steps with one subtraction." Balestriero cited an example from the paper: a boat crossing a river with a hidden current and how the JEPA world model and counterfactual world model predicted the boat's movements. "The world model predicted where the boat would end up after steering left, and it actually ended up two meters further downstream," he explained. "Those two meters are the signature of the current that occurred. To answer 'where would the boat be had I steered right?', we take the world model's prediction for steering right and add the same two meters. In other words, the counterfactual is what actually happened, plus the difference between the model's predictions for the alternative action and for the action taken. "Physics enters to justify this rule, not as an equation the model fits. Over a short time, the effect of the action and the effect of the current each grow in proportion to the duration, while their interaction grows only with its square, so over short steps they simply add up, like forces. "We prove that the fraction of the disturbance's effect that this rule gets wrong shrinks with the square of the time step, and that no method using the same ordinary data can do better than this rate in the worst case." Counterfactual world models have three likely uses, according to Balestriero. The first is having a robot retry an action after a surprise event. "When an attempt fails because of something the robot could not see, such as an unexpected load, a current or a weak motor, the counterfactual world model lets it estimate which action would have worked in that same situation," he explained. "In our simulated tasks, choosing the action this way reaches the goal in 35 to 69 percent of events, against 14 to 23 percent for a planner that knows the true simulator but ignores the observed outcome. With two more attempts on the same event, success rises to 51 to 97 percent." He also sees these models being useful for adapting to persistent fault, like a miscalibrated motor, and helping people understand failures. "World models are trained to predict, and they are getting good at it," said Balestriero. "But a robot, a car or a drone acting in the real world is constantly surprised by things it could not see coming. After a surprise, the useful question is no longer what was expected, but what would have happened had it acted differently in that same situation. You need that answer to debug a failure, to tell a bad decision from bad luck, or to decide what to do on the next attempt, and prediction alone does not provide it." The researchers initial attempts to address the issue involved adding a learned component to the world model that infers whatever hidden disturbance (e.g. the current in the boat scenario) affected the anticipated outcome. But that approach was too fragile. "Trying to understand why led to the theory," explained Balestriero. "Learning the disturbance from ordinary data is not just hard, it is impossible in general: the hidden noise can be relabeled differently for each action without changing a single prediction, so the data cannot tell you which labeling is the right one. The missing piece has to come from outside the data. For physical systems, it comes from the fact that forces add and that their interaction builds up slowly in time. Once you accept that, the learned disturbance model becomes unnecessary: the gap between what happened and what was predicted already carries the information you need, and you carry it over to the other action." Balestriero said that control engineers have been implementing corrections of this sort for decades, to tune hand-built models. "What was missing was why the same correction is justified for learned world models that see the world through images, what can and cannot be recovered without paired data, and where it breaks," he said. "The paper answers these questions by connecting causal inference, control and world models. The title comes from Leibniz's law of continuity, natura non facit saltus, 'nature makes no leaps,' which is precisely the property that makes all of this work." The researchers' paper was provided to The Register prior to its release on ArXiv. It is expected to be released this week. ® Leibniz lives! Microsoft takes three decades to make Sticky Keys less sticky
2026-11-09

Microsoft takes three decades to make Sticky Keys less sticky

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Windows preview adds a 'don't ask again' option to accessibility prompts, sparing users a trip through Settings Windows testers are getting a new option in the Sticky Keys and Filter Keys dialogs – one to banish the pop-ups without needing to venture deep into Settings. It's only taken several decades for Microsoft to put the off switch where the interruption actually happens. The accessibility features themselves are useful, though triggering their activation prompts by accident is another matter. Once enabled, Sticky Keys let users press modifier keys (such as Control) one at a time instead of holding them down together. For example, rather than holding Control and pressing C to copy, a user can press and release Control, then press C. Filter Keys, meanwhile, ignores brief or repeated keystrokes and adjusts the keyboard's repeat behavior. However, the dialogs are of little use to people who don't need the features, and they can pop up at the most inconvenient moments. Tap Shift five times, and you'll see what we mean: Windows chimes and shows a dialog asking whether you'd like to turn on Sticky Keys. Dismiss it, hit Shift five times, and there it is again. Or hold down the right Shift key for eight seconds to be treated to the Filter Keys pop-up. You can stop this behavior elsewhere in the system, but it requires a trek through Windows settings. The change, introduced in the Experimental Windows Insider Build 26340.9882, adds an option to the Sticky Keys or Filter Keys dialogs to dismiss the prompts permanently. It's a small change, but one that should spare users an irritating interruption. Better late than never – even if "never ask me again" has taken roughly three decades to reach the dialog. Former Microsoft engineer Charles Opperman said he wrote the dialog in early 1995 "after @bradsilverberg kids kept activating StickeyKeys [sic] while playing Space Cadet pinball." Opperman added that a link to Settings was added in Windows 98 or what would become Windows 2000, and a checkbox was considered even then, but it wasn't deemed a priority. Users could turn off the prompt via settings. Now the option is front and center. The option is also rolling out in the Windows Insider Beta channel, although Microsoft has not announced a general release date. ® Leibniz lives! NASA keeps its PROMISE to raid Mars testbeds for parts
2026-11-09

NASA keeps its PROMISE to raid Mars testbeds for parts

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Cut-and-shut trundlebot with spiffy new paint job meant to be headed to the Moon in 2028 NASA is moving ahead with its plan to repurpose a Mars rover engineering model as a lunar trundlebot for less than $400 million. The robot, dubbed PROMISE (Polar Rover for Observation, Mapping, and In-Situ Exploration), is a cut-and-shut of engineering test articles used for the Perseverance and Curiosity rovers, currently rolling around the Red Planet. During a weekend briefing, NASA outlined the plan for PROMISE: to land the robot on the lunar surface by late 2028 for an 18-month mission. PROMISE will be preceded by NASA's VIPER trundlebot, which is expected to survive only 100 days on the Moon's surface. PROMISE will carry copies of VIPER's science instruments, including a drill capable of boring 1 meter into the lunar regolith. Power will come from a Radioisotope Thermoelectric Generator (RTG) left over from the Cassini mission. NASA acknowledged the fuel was processed some time ago – the Cassini mission to Saturn was launched in 1997 – but anticipates there will be plenty of heat and power for PROMISE's components. Blue Origin is to deposit the rover on the Moon. NASA awarded the company a $94 million base contract, "with an option period worth an additional $140 million based on performance to deliver PROMISE to the lunar South Pole." The figures make it difficult to ascertain the exact cost of the PROMISE mission. However, during the briefing, NASA said it had "a pretty firm agreement with JPL to basically deliver the rover to the surface under $400 million." The $400 million includes the lander, which will be Blue Origin's Mark 1. Earlier this year, The Planetary Society's chief of space policy, Casey Drier, estimated that the mission (including repurposing the hardware developed for Mars and landing it on the Moon) would cost at least $723 million. At the time, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman said: "if PROMISE costs even 20 percent of your low-end estimate, we simply will not fly it." Assuming Isaacman was only talking about the rover, Drier's figure drops to $489 million without launch and landing costs. 20 percent of that is… not going to be enough. That also doesn't account for the impact of taking away the hardware built for the Mars program. During the briefing, NASA said it was "pretty comfortable" with how the Mars rovers were going and so was happy about repurposing the hardware or, as one speaker put it, "can we give it a new paint job?" A source told The Register that morale at the agency's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) was not high at present. Raiding successful programs to fulfill the administration's whims is unlikely to have improved the mood of engineers. Still. Another trundlebot on the Moon. Far better than using that spare RTG for yet another deep space mission, right? ® Leibniz lives! Microsoft's Azure Linux gets 470-plus security fixes in one almighty patching session
2026-11-09

Microsoft's Azure Linux gets 470-plus security fixes in one almighty patching session

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
A mountain of CVEs, a certificate cleanup, and yet more work for Microsoft's patching teams Microsoft has unleashed a whopper of an update for Azure Linux, packing more than 470 CVE fixes into a single release as the software industry's ever-growing pile of vulnerabilities shows little sign of shrinking. Over the weekend, Microsoft rolled out Azure Linux 3.0.2022 with fixes covering everything from the Linux kernel to open source packages and container software. The changelog runs to hundreds of entries, so anyone planning to read the whole thing might want to put the kettle on. Microsoft's release notes contain more than 470 CVE references, although some vulnerabilities appear multiple times because they affect different packages. So that's not necessarily 470 separate security holes, but it's still an impressive amount of patching for one release. The fixes span OpenSSL, curl, Kubernetes, Docker, QEMU, and a lengthy list of other packages that underpin modern Linux environments. The generic Linux kernel has been updated to version 6.6.161.1. There's also been some housekeeping following the eMudhra certificate incident, tracked as CVE-2026-114406. Microsoft has removed or distrusted root certificates that could validate compromised eMudhra intermediate certificates, while adding checks in Python and Go to reject the affected certificates. It has also preserved SSL.com TLS trust where required, presumably to avoid fixing one certificate headache by creating another. Container infrastructure gets plenty of attention, too, with fixes spanning KubeVirt, containerd, Docker Compose, and other components used to keep cloud workloads running. Several CVEs crop up repeatedly across these packages, helping explain the eye-watering number of entries in the changelog. The release includes less security-focused changes too, including new Dockerfiles, an OpenVMM patch for confidential computing support, fixes for systemd hangs, and adjustments to GRUB Secure Boot metadata. The hefty release comes amid an AI-fueled surge in vulnerability discovery that has left developers struggling to keep up with the resulting patch workload. As The Register reported earlier today, Linux creator Linus Torvalds reckons there may finally be signs that the flood is easing, at least in some corners of the kernel. Torvalds said fixes are now arriving faster than new bug reports in the kernel's eBPF subsystem, although he noted that most other areas haven't reached that point. Whether the wider software industry is anywhere close to catching up is another matter, and Microsoft hasn't said if AI-assisted research contributed to the vulnerabilities addressed in this release. Azure Linux, formerly known as CBL-Mariner, is Microsoft's own Linux distribution, developed primarily for its cloud infrastructure and services. That makes keeping its many components patched rather more important than the modest version-number bump might suggest. And judging by the size of this latest changelog, Microsoft's patching teams aren't likely to be putting their feet up anytime soon. ® Leibniz lives! Who Gives A Crap? Hackers, apparently, after toilet paper maker's customer data exposed
2026-11-09

Who Gives A Crap? Hackers, apparently, after toilet paper maker's customer data exposed

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
UK shoppers caught up in third-party security mess, and nobody knows whether the data was flushed away Toilet paper purveyor Who Gives A Crap has found itself in a bit of a mess after an intruder accessed customer information through a third-party software provider, leaving the company scrambling to clean up a breach affecting shoppers in the UK, US, and Australia. In an email sent to affected customers over the weekend, seen by The Register, the loo roll seller warns that personal information was accessed without authorization after a security incident at an unnamed US-based marketing software provider. The email explains that the intruder gained access to the supplier's systems on October 30. The provider spotted the intrusion the following day and says it secured its systems by November 1, although Who Gives A Crap wasn't told until November 6. It's unclear why the supplier sat on the bad news for another five days before passing it on. The exposed information includes customers' names, email addresses, phone numbers, mailing addresses, and total spending with the company. In other words, someone may now know not only where you live, but just how much of your hard-earned cash you've flushed down the crapper. Payment information, order details, and passwords were not involved, and customers' accounts were not directly accessed. That's something of a relief, although whether the intruder actually made off with the data remains a pressing question. "We don't yet know whether the information was copied or downloaded," the company told customers, adding that it had found no evidence of misuse. In a separate public statement, Who Gives A Crap co-founder and CEO Simon Griffiths confirmed that the incident affected customers across the company's Australian, UK, and US stores. Canadian customers, meanwhile, appear to have escaped this particular toilet emergency, with the supplier reporting that no personal information belonging to shoppers in the country was accessed. Who Gives A Crap says it has since disconnected the provider from its systems, changed access credentials, and begun warning affected customers to watch out for suspicious emails, texts, calls, and other unsolicited communications. "The main concern is scam or phishing messages that use your real details to look convincing," the company warned in its customer email. While Who Gives A Crap has been busy cleaning up, the supplier at the center of this particular stink remains a mystery. The company hasn't identified the software provider or disclosed how many customers were affected, including those in the UK, and didn’t respond to our questions. Griffiths said the company is "engaging with the relevant regulatory authorities, as required." In the UK, the ICO told The Register: "We can confirm Who Gives A Crap Limited has reported an incident and we are assessing the information provided." Who Gives A Crap has plenty of toilet paper to hand, although cleaning up this particular situation might require more than a few rolls. ® Leibniz lives! www.theregister.com - Articles
The AI bubble can wait, but your exit plan can't
2026-11-09

The AI bubble can wait, but your exit plan can't

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Infrastructure spending outruns measurable returns, with supplier finances key to judging when trouble arrives The AI bubble isn't about to burst, Forrester reckons, although businesses should prepare for a correction that could upend their suppliers, contracts, and prices. Spending on infrastructure has raced ahead of measurable returns, the analyst says, yet the financial strength of the companies funding the build-out is what keep Forrester from predicting an imminent crash. "Fears that an AI bubble is about to burst are premature," said Forrester emerging technology veep Brian Hopkins. "About nine-tenths of the money building today's AI computing infrastructure comes from large, healthy technology companies paying out of their own cash flow. The risk is concentrated in a few places: a small number of builders under real financial strain, and circular financing deals with the AI labs." Conveniently, Forrester has developed an AI Bubble Model to help technology leaders monitor the risks and prepare. Its latest report, "An AI Bubble Burst Is Not Imminent," notes what many in the IT industry, including The Register, have been saying for a while: that the hyperscale operators are committing a record share of revenue into capital expenditure to bring online more infrastructure to support AI development and use. At the same time, the boost in productivity doesn't justify this supplier capacity build-out. Forrester calculates that the infrastructure investments break even only if AI raises US GDP by 5 percent by 2035. Published estimates suggest productivity improvements could provide about two percentage points of that uplift, leaving roughly $1.3 trillion to come from other sources of value. A recent report from management consultants Bain & Company came to similar conclusions, estimating the AI industry needs to generate $6 trillion in annual revenue by 2031 to keep funding all the infrastructure it needs, and this will require new uses beyond simply enhancing employee productivity. Forrester also claims that the forces making AI abundant also make parts of the market unstable, and says that enterprise users should take heed of how prices collapsed as major providers like Anthropic slashed token costs in response to competition from open source models. If demand disappoints as new capacity comes online, suppliers could cut investment, merge, change prices, or discontinue products. Customers could find themselves rebuilding AI plans around suppliers and services that no longer exist. Forrester advises businesses to track whether their AI projects deliver measurable value and limit their exposure to vulnerable suppliers – with its help, of course. Companies can judge how things are going by looking at the gap between suppliers' contracted business and revenue actually recognized, along with how they fund their build-outs. If a vendor leans more on outside investment and customer prepayments, as Oracle and CoreWeave have, this is a red flag to cut back dependencies on them, Forrester says. Enterprises should also stress-test AI plans against scenarios such as a key vendor announcing end of support in 12 months, and an AI model supplier defaulting on payments to its compute vendor and cutting back service-level agreements for enterprise workloads. Companies should know in advance which AI use cases to drop, what data and workloads need to be moved, and at what cost. But Hopkins sounded a further note of caution: "Oversupply on its own won't decide whether a correction comes. Demand will, and that's the biggest unknown, because it depends on enterprises finding lasting value in AI beyond simple productivity gains." Organizations shouldn't pull back, he insists. They should keep investing where AI delivers value that can be demonstrated, closely monitor the financial health of the companies building AI computing, and be ready for corrections. Recently, the International Monetary Fund started ringing alarm bells that financial markets are increasingly exposed to an AI-driven correction that could lead to widespread economic disruption. Earlier this year, the Bank for International Settlements also raised concerns that the current situation with the AI industry bore the hallmarks of historical economic bubbles and presented a risk to global economic stability. ® Leibniz lives! That was before she became “an executive” at Megaspeed, which bought $4.8 billion of hard-to-get Nvidia chips, starting around the time she was being photographed with Jensen in a Taipei bar in 2024. “To many in the industry, the well-funded push by Megaspeed into A.I. appeared to come out of nowhere,” The New York Times wrote, tactfully. Uber wants to park your car now, too
2026-11-09

Uber wants to park your car now, too

The Verge | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Powered by its SpotHero acquisition, Uber’s new parking reservation tool is a play for commuter loyalty — and a staging ground for its robotaxi future. Powered by its SpotHero acquisition, Uber’s new parking reservation tool is a play for commuter loyalty — and a staging ground for its robotaxi future. Uber announced the launch of Uber Parking, a native in-app reservation feature that allows drivers to find, book, and pay for garage and lot spaces ahead of time. The new feature is a result of Uber’s recent acquisition of SpotHero, integrating a network of over 13,000 facilities across more than 400 cities into the company’s platform. The new feature works by tapping the new “Parking” icon in the Uber app to view an interactive map with upfront prices and arrival times. You can filter spots by self-park or valet, outdoor lot or garage, and whether staff are on-site. The app will prompt you to enter your vehicle details and license plate, after which can enter certain lots using a QR code on your phone or through an automatic license plate scan. If your plans change, you can extend your time directly in the app or cancel for free right up until your reservation starts. Uber One subscribers can earn 10 percent back on parking reservations as account credits within 24 hours. These credits can then be spent on rides or Uber Eats deliveries. Uber says that its subscription service has grown to more than 55 million members across 47 countries as of late 2026. Uber Parking is launching in 15 initial markets, including Chicago, NYC, Boston, San Francisco, Los Angeles, Washington D.C., Atlanta, Austin, Dallas, Denver, Miami, Nashville, Philadelphia, San Diego, and Seattle — eventually rolling out to SpotHero’s existing 400-city footprint. The move also ties directly into Uber’s wider bet on robotaxis, working with over 30 autonomous vehicle partners across multiple cities. In the long run, these 13,000-plus parking facilities won’t just hold commuter cars — Uber plans to turn them into urban holding pens and waiting areas for driverless robotaxis. Stashing autonomous fleets off the street when demand dips helps keep empty cars from aimlessly circling city blocks, cutting down on traffic, emissions, and wear on the vehicles, Uber says. Ultimately, it shows that the company is thinking bigger than just ride-hailing and delivery — it wants to control the city’s physical roads and parking spaces, regardless of whether the person traveling is driving their own car, riding in the backseat, or letting a computer take the wheel. By getting into parking, Uber is also tacitly admitting that its earlier mission to end personal car ownership and driver’s licenses didn’t succeed. But there is a loyalty-retaining strategy at play here, too, with Uber intent on keeping its customers operating within in its own platform ecosystem, whether they’re hailing a ride or driving their own car. MIT Technology Review
Nancy Pelosi’s replacement is ready to regulate AI
2026-11-09

Nancy Pelosi’s replacement is ready to regulate AI

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
As a California state senator, Scott Wiener authored legislation aimed at curbing the risks of advanced AI. Now, after winning a tight congressional race for former Speaker of the House Nancy Pelosi’s open San Francisco seat, he could play a key role in setting federal AI policy. I spoke with Wiener on Friday, only a few hours after he had been declared the victor. But he was already thinking about AI risk. The previous day, the AI nonprofit METR had released a report that had been widely interpreted as evidence that Anthropic had achieved recursive self-improvement, a phenomenon in which AI meaningfully contributes to its own advancement and AI progress potentially spins out of control. “It’s very concerning,” Wiener said, citing the report as evidence that we are quickly running out of time to regulate AI. “It was my hope that Congress would take decisive action before [the end of] this year,” he said. “That does not appear to be happening.” Wiener has been increasingly focused on AI in recent years, and he’s made many of the AI safety movement’s concerns his own: During our 20-minute conversation, he used the phrase “recursive self-improvement” half a dozen times, for example.“We need to slow this down,” he says. “Because it’s moving very quickly, and we could sleepwalk into a disaster.” Wiener has already had real influence on AI policy. SB 1047, a bill that he authored in 2024, was set to become the first real piece of frontier AI legislation in the country before it was vetoed by California governor Gavin Newsom; he then sponsored SB 53, a lighter-touch bill that Newsom signed. Both bills have inspired proposed federal legislation. Wiener’s congressional campaign received over $3 million from Public First Action, a nonprofit in favor of AI regulation that receives substantial funding from Anthropic. That contribution led opponents to criticize him for being too close to the AI industry, but Wiener says that couldn’t be further from the truth. “Public First supported me because I was the first lawmaker in the country to introduce AI regulation and the first to pass it and get it implemented,” he wrote in a message after our interview. “The attacks on me for [Public First’s] support were shockingly cynical—literally attacking a candidate who actually regulated AI over Big Tech’s objection and falsely claiming the candidate is somehow too close to Big Tech.” Wiener says that if Congress forms a select committee on artificial intelligence—something that Democrats have discussed since September—he’d be eager to serve. And he’s enthusiastic about many of the policy ideas floating around Congress. “I think it’s really important to have a strong, empowered, nimble federal regulator,” he says, noting that Representative Ro Khanna, a fellow Democrat from California, has proposed legislation to establish such a regulator. He also highlights the importance of whistleblower protections, testing and auditing requirements, and a kill switch requirement, and he wants to see Congress leverage its oversight powers to hold the AI labs to account. “This is about the future of humanity, and we want to make sure that the public has a say and that the public knows what's going on,” he says. And as a longtime state legislator, he is opposed to broad federal preemption of state AI laws, which some of the proposed federal legislation would require. “States are, as Brandeis says, laboratories,” he notes. “You want the states to be able to continue to be nimble in ways that Congress sometimes is not.” Above all, Wiener says, he wants Congress to pass policies with real teeth. “It’s critically important that when Congress acts, that we do so in a way that is truly meaningful and that meets the moment,” he says. That means taking seriously the possibility of AI catastrophe, something he discusses in the sort of language typically used by AI safety experts. “We’re in a moment that is very dangerous in terms of the risk of loss of control over AI,” he told me. “We need to deal with recursive self-improvement. Once you’re in that realm, that really is potentially a recipe for total loss of control.” He’ll have some allies in the fight against catastrophic AI risk. Senator Bernie Sanders, for instance, has met with many of the AI safety movement’s most influential figures and has spoken repeatedly about AI and existential risk. But there’s still a large gap between Wiener’s Bay Area, AGI-pilled perspective and the national mood on AI, which is anchored by opposition to data centers. The fact that voters approved 40 out of 41 anti-data-center ballot measures on Tuesday might seem to be a good sign for Wiener and others who support AI regulation, but Wiener says he sees this movement as far more tied to local concerns than to bigger questions about AI risk. “Peoople who are really opposed to where AI is going—this is an area where they can actually do something about it,” he says. “But the data center revolt—to me, I think it’s more so that people are upset that there could be something like this in their community, with all the impacts near people’s homes, driving up electric bills and using water. I think that’s the bigger issue for most people, from what I can tell.” Wiener describes himself as only “cautiously optimistic” about Congress’s ability to rein in AI, given its poor track record on regulating the tech industry. He also has concerns about the ability to get legislation past President Donald Trump, who called concerns about catastrophic AI risk a “hoax” in September and has demonstrated a stark unwillingness to collaborate with China, something that Wiener sees as essential to preventing the worst consequences of AI. But he says Trump’s unpredictability provides  some measure of hope, though it’s a fraught kind of hope. “So far what we’ve seen are some problematic things, but not complete disasters,” Wiener says. “I hope that it doesn’t take a disaster to spur the president into acting. And I hope if that’s what it does take—which, like I said, I hope it doesn’t—but if it does, I hope it’s as small as possible.” Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. An exclusive interview with Nick Cammarata, the researcher whose 26,000-word viral blog post has AI safety folks celebrating. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. With the rise of personal AI assistants, the internet isn't just for humans anymore. Tropical forests are our planet’s biggest terrestrial carbon sink, accounting for around 40% of carbon stored on land. Forest ecologist Daisy Dent, a senior scientist at ETH Zurich, is part of an international network of researchers working to decipher the networks of animal and plant interactions that enable some forests to recover after destruction. The goal is a low-cost, high-impact alternative to typical carbon-­crediting schemes, which plant huge areas with a single species of tree. For over a decade, her research has helped show that these large “monocrop” programs could be expensive mistakes—and that more resilient forests can rebound naturally in just a few decades, re-creating ecosystems that suck up vast quantities of carbon dioxide. Learn more about Dent, her work, and her impact on how we understand the role rainforests play in regulating our climate.
