Innovating with AI in the special chemicals industry
2026-11-09

Innovating with AI in the special chemicals industry

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
As companies look to accelerate innovation with artificial intelligence, cloud computing, and advanced modeling, the primary challenge is connecting new technologies to reliable data, scientific expertise, and the practical realities of bringing products to market. For specialty chemicals manufacturer Lubrizol, digital transformation is all about giving researchers the tools to test ideas faster, make better-informed decisions, and develop more sustainable materials without compromising performance. Girish Rao, vice president of information technology at Lubrizol, says the company is building a digital foundation that connects business processes, modern data platforms, and cloud infrastructure to support its research and development (R&D) teams. Their goal is to combine decades of accumulated technical data with the deep expertise of chemists and engineers who understand customer needs as well as the physical constraints of their work. Data scientists can help identify patterns, test new hypotheses, and model possibilities before researchers conduct physical experiments. But, Rao emphasizes, “it's not about replacing scientific judgment; it is about giving our scientists better tools.” Scientists, data scientists, and IT teams approach problems from different perspectives, prioritizing experimental validity, model performance, and platform security and scalability, respectively. By bringing these groups together in “fusion teams,” Lubrizol aligns them around shared objectives, starting with small pilots to build trust. "Once the trust is built between the team members and they know what value they're about to create," Rao says, "things go a lot better." Cloud infrastructure also gives the company flexibility to access computing resources and modern tools without making the substantial investments required to build and maintain its own data centers. These capabilities could prove especially valuable in advancing sustainability. Digital modeling can help researchers evaluate more formulations, explore materials such as starch and corn-based alternatives, and assess trade-offs across a product's life cycle. Promising a material in a laboratory is only the first step. Scaling production while meeting performance expectations, regulatory requirements, and financial constraints is still a tightrope walk, but AI's ability to model multiple variables could help teams navigate those competing demands. Looking to the future, Rao expects AI, automation, advanced modeling, digital twins, and connected data to converge as laboratories become more intelligent and manufacturing plants more efficient. The opportunity, he suggests, lies in bringing these capabilities together responsibly and at scale. “It's not just a single technology. I think it's the combination of all of these, and it'll be the ability to connect all these processes that are going to generate tremendous amounts of data." The climate case for AI has not been made
2026-11-09

The climate case for AI has not been made

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
This week, as countries meet in Antalya, Turkey for the United Nations climate conference, one topic likely to dominate the conversation is artificial intelligence. The host has already called on countries to commit to ensuring that AI is designed, powered, and deployed in ways that support climate action. But so far, industries around the world aren’t doing nearly enough to harness the technology to actually drive down the greenhouse-gas emissions heating up the planet, despite some very optimistic claims from AI companies. In 2024, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman declared that AI would unleash “astounding triumphs,” including “fixing the climate.” The year before, in a 2023 op-ed, the head of sustainability at Google and her coauthor wrote that AI could reduce global emissions 5% to 10% by 2030. Some academic researchers have examined these AI-related climate claims. A peer-reviewed study published last year, led by researchers at the London School of Economics’ Grantham Research Institute and the firm Systemiq, estimated that AI could help cut global emissions by the equivalent of 3.2 to 5.4 billion tons of carbon dioxide annually by 2035—roughly on par with the US’s annual energy-related emissions on the high end, even after subtracting AI’s own energy footprint. Those potential gains are mostly concentrated in three sectors: power, meat and dairy, and vehicles. But projections like these are only as good as our willingness to test them against present-day reality. So is there evidence that the largest companies in these three sectors are beginning to integrate AI in line with the study’s projections, and if so, do the results match expectations? Or is the 2035 number still mostly aspirational? Companies across these sectors have announced plenty of initial efforts to incorporate AI into various business practices. But an AI-assisted review of public information from significant market actors in each sector, conducted and reviewed by the second author of this piece, found that few have announced they’re pursuing the specific emissions-reducing routes that informed the projections in the study—and fewer still claim they’ve used them to achieve meaningful climate progress. The power sector is where the earlier study’s underlying math is most concrete: AI-driven forecasting of supply and demand could increase the amount of renewable electricity that reaches the grid from already-built solar and wind facilities by up to 20%, which could prevent an estimated 1.8 billion tons of emissions a year. Yet of the grid operators reviewed in China, the United States, and Europe, only one deployed a known meaningful, working AI program of this sort, according to the analysis. A partnership between the UK’s National Grid ESO and Open Climate Fix halved the grid operator’s solar-forecast error (the difference between the amount of incoming sunlight predicted and the amount that actually reaches solar panels), avoiding hundreds of thousands of tons of carbon dioxide a year. Beyond this example in the UK, though, many other grid operators in the EU, the US, and China had little to show in the public record in terms of the AI integration envisioned by the study. The meat and dairy sector offers the starkest contrast between technology readiness and market reality. To achieve the 2035 emission reductions anticipated in the earlier study, AI would need to close the taste and cost gap between traditional proteins and alternative ones, such as lab-grown or plant-based burgers and milk substitutes. In one of the clearest efforts to achieve this, the AI and food tech company NotCo recently formed a joint venture with Kraft Heinz using its platform Giuseppe, which analyzes plant molecular data to pinpoint combinations replicating the taste, texture, and smell of animal products. But few other alternative-protein companies had such a deeply integrated way of using AI in product development, the AI-assisted review suggests. Meanwhile, the sector seems to be facing more fundamental problems, including stubbornly slow mainstream consumer adoption and declining funding levels. Most notably, venture capital flowing into cultivated meat fell from a $989 million peak in 2021 to around $83 million last year. The transportation sector offers some hopeful examples of the type of AI integration necessary to achieve the study’s projections, particularly among battery manufacturers and ride-sharing platforms. CATL—which controls roughly 40% of the global market for electric-vehicle batteries—launched an AI system trained on more than 100,000 battery design cases and 600 terabytes of test data. It cut the time needed to screen candidate materials by two orders of magnitude, according to the company. Independent researchers also found that the ride-sharing platform Didi Chuxing was able cut per-trip emissions 16% on average by using AI-optimized routing to minimize detours. There’s another big problem in the climate case for AI, though: Oil and gas companies are also interested in these tools for many reasons, including the potential to increase production of fossil fuels. Technology firms that committed to sustainability goals in the past should answer questions about how their tools could be used to boost old, dirty energy as well as clean, renewable energy. Finally, it’s also worth noting that integrating AI is just the first step, and it remains unclear to what degree the technology can accelerate the sorts of improvements and innovations envisioned. AI also does little to shorten the standard paths to certifying, permitting, building, packaging, marketing, and selling any resulting products, facilities, or services. The findings of the analysis, carried out by Anthropic’s Claude Code, were reviewed against public documents and media accounts to ensure their accuracy. It is possible that some companies and utilities in these industries are pursuing AI-assisted applications that would fit into the initial study’s scenarios but haven’t yet been disclosed publicly. Greater and better-targeted AI integration and efficiency gains in these three sectors may well materialize, bringing the 2035 projections closer to reality. But it won’t happen without deliberate and sustained efforts to ensure that AI delivers more environmental benefits than harms. Notably, so far the rapid adoption of AI models and buildout of data centers has driven a huge increase in energy consumption and emissions, and this has led major tech companies like Microsoft, Google, and others to fall well off track in their corporate climate goals. At this point it would be surprising if the sector even manages to zero out its climate pollution: Emissions associated with global data centers (which may handle AI as well as non-AI computing tasks) could exceed 300 million tons annually in 2035, according to the IEA’s base forecast. Industry leaders must implement the technology in the ways that promise to do the most to drive down emissions. Corporate customers should select models with the lowest environmental impacts and opt to work with the AI developers doing the most to reduce their climate footprint. And states and regions need to do more to incentivize or require all these companies to operate in more responsible ways. Mary Nichols is distinguished counsel at the UCLA School of Law and the former chair of the California Air Resources Board. Jeremy Tamanini is the founder of Dual Citizen, a sustainability research and advisory firm. These scientists hope that fire-spotting satellites, AI tools and ancient land practices could preserve the world’s vulnerable permafrost—and the vast carbon it stores. New technology could help cut power losses and reinvent a staple of the grid. MIT Technology Review
I watched a humanoid robot clean my apartment. It felt like a taste of the future.
2026-11-06

I watched a humanoid robot clean my apartment. It felt like a taste of the future.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
I’m not the sort of person who typically pays for a house cleaner. I’m too frugal, and I kind of enjoy putting on a podcast and going to town on my tub with a Magic Eraser. But when I saw an X post about a new humanoid-robot cleaning service in San Francisco, I immediately signed up for the waitlist. I wanted my apartment to feel like The Jetsons, even if just for an hour. I was also motivated by journalistic interest. In order to ensure an authentic experience, I didn’t tell Tau Robotics, the startup behind the X post, that I was a journalist—though I did use my real name, so it’s possible someone looked me up. Tau’s long-term goal is to make autonomous cleaning robots a reality, but they’re not quite there yet: For now, the robots are piloted remotely by humans, who watch what’s going on via cameras mounted in the robots’ heads and wrists. As the robots clean, they collect data that Tau will use to train up their more independent descendants. Every counter successfully wiped and T-shirt successfully folded will help the models that power the robots learn to do better on the next go-round. Because this data is so valuable to Tau, the company charges less for cleaning than one might expect for such a cutting-edge product. At the time I booked, Tau was offering $30 for an hourlong cleaning with a single robot; now the charge is $60 per weekly cleaning with two robots, or $100 for a one-time visit. But even at the cheaper rate of $30 per hour, Tau’s robots aren’t a great bargain. They are thorough but slow, so if you, like me, are tempted to stand around watching them for their entire visit, it would probably be a better use of your time to just take care of the cleaning yourself. But the robots are physically capable of cleaning, and they’re undeniably cute. Tau has armed each four-and-a-half-foot-tall robot with a 3D-printed “head” with antennae that allow it to communicate with Tau’s headquarters, as well as two 3D-printed claw “hands” (the more humanlike alternatives apparently aren’t durable enough, and claws do surprisingly well for most cleaning tasks). Mine was wearing a Tau Robotics shirt, and grippy socks so that it didn’t scratch hardwood floors. With all the human support they need, the robots are closer to radio-controlled cars than Waymos. Many tasks, such as making the bed and folding laundry, require what’s called kinematic guidance: The human pilot performs the necessary motions, and sensors transmit those movements to the robot, which copies them. These kinematically guided tasks produce especially valuable training data. Tau’s AI models might not have solved laundry folding yet, but learning from the examples of robots that have completed the task in the real world is how they’ll get there. But according to Tau cofounder Cornelia Weinzierl, to whom I came clean as a journalist a few weeks after the cleaning, the robot can already complete some other tasks—such as vacuuming and mopping—without any human help. If Tau succeeds in using all the data it is collecting to train truly autonomous cleaning robots, they could even become San Francisco’s newest tech tourist attraction as Waymos become more commonplace countrywide. The cleaning robot that came to my building last month was already a bit of a spectacle. It arrived in a blue minivan, out of which it was helped by Weinzierl and another Tau employee. That’s when the robot encountered its first obstacle: the eight stairs leading up to the building’s front door. I’d come down to greet Weinzierl and the robot, so I had a front-row seat to its struggles with my steps. It climbed as if it were drunk. Each successful step was as likely as not to be followed by a stumbling backward stagger that erased all its progress—though, impressively, it never entirely lost its footing. When I later discussed my experience with Weinzierl, she explained that the robot sometimes walks strangely because it is operated remotely from Tau’s headquarters, and the short signal transmission delay can affect its coordination. “The amount of work and time that we spend just optimizing, for example, timings and connectivity is crazy,” she said. Inside, the carpeted staircase leading up to my apartment defeated the robot entirely, and Weinzierl had to lug it up the last few steps. This, it turned out, was not typical. As the robot cleaned, it repeatedly stalled out, something Weinzierl attributed to it overheating in the unseasonably hot weather—until she heard from one of her colleagues that the heat had shut down not the robot but the equipment that its human controller was using to watch its progress through my apartment. Without remote assistance, the robot couldn’t clean, and so it, and Weinzierl, departed before my booked hour was up. (Back in September, Weinzierl or another Tau representative remained with the robot throughout its visit, but she says that’s no longer necessary.) You can’t fault Tau on customer service, though. Weinzierl quickly emailed me to apologize, comp the visit, and offer me another on the house (minus whatever price I assign to my data privacy)—and that was all before I told her I was a journalist. I scheduled the next cleaning for about a month later, after a couple of trips out of town, and it went much more smoothly—the robot got up my stairs without much assistance, though still with the drunken stagger. In the hour it was there, it did a creditable job of cleaning my kitchen (though it didn’t brave the stove) and even saved me my most dreaded cleaning task, the toilet. That was its most impressive performance: Three times the head fell off its toilet cleaning wand, and three times it managed to reattach it by poking around with the handle until it made contact at just the right angle. The robot is still rough around the edges, which is to be expected for a product in beta. It repeatedly left my taps dripping, it knocked over a (closed) bottle of olive oil, and it couldn’t manage to right an air purifier that it had toppled. But I easily forgave all those missteps because of how charming it was. As I watched it wipe down my countertops, I felt the same fond gratitude that I feel when a Waymo delivers me safely home after a night out. Still, it’s not all slapstick and whimsy. If Tau Robotics or its competitors succeeds in training robots to clean autonomously and inexpensively, people could lose their livelihoods, and the United States is a long way away from implementing policies to take care of those left unemployed by AI. But Weinzierl maintains that someday turning over cleaning work to robots could make a lot of sense. “Cleaning is not a job that a lot of people really actively want to be doing—there’s high turnover, there’s a high risk of injury,” she said when we spoke after the cleanings. “We need people, and we don’t have them, and no one wants to do it.” She said that the company is also starting to consider someday deploying the robots for other dangerous or physically challenging jobs, such as mold remediation and home health care. Tau Robotics still has a long way to go: The amount of data needed to train robots to do the varied and complicated tasks of apartment cleaning is probably enormous, and the company has completed only a couple hundred cleanings so far. But it’s remarkable that there are now humanoid robots able to clean your toilets and mop your floors, even if they need quite a bit of human help to do so. The Jetsons no longer feels quite so far away. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. With the rise of personal AI assistants, the internet isn't just for humans anymore. Adobe becomes Disney’s AI partner to build models on its characters
2026-11-09

Adobe becomes Disney’s AI partner to build models on its characters

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
The Alameda Avenue entrance to the Walt Disney Studios in Burbank, California, in July 2016. Adobe is now Disney’s “Official AI Innovation Partner” under a multi-year deal to build custom AI models trained on Disney’s characters, settings and styles. It extends work with Walt Disney Imagineering to Disney’s studio and ESPN. No financial terms were disclosed. Adobe has become Disney’s “Official AI Innovation Partner” in a multi-year deal to build custom AI models around Disney’s characters, the companies said on Monday. They announced it at Adobe MAX, its creativity conference in Miami Beach. Neither disclosed financial terms. Disney gets early access to Adobe tools such as Firefly, Photoshop and Premiere, plus its own team of Adobe engineers. With Firefly Foundry, Adobe’s tool for building private models, the two will train AI on Disney’s characters, worlds and art styles. Adobe says the results will be “commercially safe,” though Firefly itself now also offers OpenAI’s image models. The deal widens work that began at Walt Disney Imagineering, which designs Disney’s theme parks. In June, Adobe showed Imagineering tools that turn rough sketches into concept art and create images for franchises such as Frozen and Moana. Disney said those models were built on licensed and Disney-owned work. Adobe will also join StudioLAB, the test lab of Disney’s film studio. It will work with ESPN on using AI with fans and the sports network’s archive. Much of the rest is marketing, with a bigger Adobe presence on Disney’s media and promotions at its parks and on its ships. Hannah Elsakr, Adobe’s vice president of generative AI new business ventures, called the deal “the next chapter of the relationship,” with AI used “as an accelerant for artistic vision.” It is Disney’s latest move in AI, after it announced an AI ad tool in June. It licensed more than 200 characters to OpenAI’s Sora video app in December 2025, then unwound the deal when OpenAI shut Sora down in March. A court filing last month showed Disney plans to spend about $180M over three years on Character.AI staff it is hiring. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Uber adds parking reservations to its app, with robotaxis in mind

Uber adds parking reservations to its app, with robotaxis in mind

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
A person holds a smartphone showing the Uber app’s loading screen. Uber launched parking reservations in 15 US cities, using the network of SpotHero, which it agreed to buy in February. The feature targets commuters, but Uber says parking spots near riders could also cut costs for robotaxi fleets on its platform. Uber now lets people reserve parking spots in its app, its first parking product since it agreed to buy SpotHero in February, the company said on Monday, Bloomberg reported. The feature launches in 15 US cities, including New York, Chicago and San Francisco, with more to follow. Users tap a “P” icon to find garages, lots and valets for a set date and time, then pay through the app. SpotHero gives Uber more than 13,000 parking sites in North America. Uber will keep SpotHero running as a separate app. Uber One members get 10% back in credits on eligible bookings, to spend on rides and food orders. Chief executive Dara Khosrowshahi told analysts last week that parking will help Uber win commuters, according to TechCrunch. “Commute is one of our largest categories,” he said. Uber says the deal could also help its robotaxi plans. It works with more than a dozen robotaxi and delivery robot makers, managing their dispatch, charging and maintenance. “The parking play is all around reducing the cost per mile for the customer,” said Samarth Kejriwal, Uber’s senior director of autonomous vehicles. A robotaxi that waits in a car park near busy areas, instead of driving back to a depot, spends less time empty, Kejriwal told Bloomberg. Uber already has parking deals for some partners. SpotHero’s contracts with US parking firms could help it find cheaper rates as robotaxis reach more cities, he said. Uber has pitched itself as the marketplace robotaxi firms plug into, though Waymo is pulling back in two cities. Its partners include Rivian, Nuro and Wayve, whose cars it began running in London in September. It is also buying beyond ride-hailing, including a $14.8B Delivery Hero takeover. “At the end of the day, the rider will care about price and reliability,” Kejriwal said. Google unveils TPU 8tx and 8i Max chips for secure and long-context AI
2026-11-09

Google unveils TPU 8tx and 8i Max chips for secure and long-context AI

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Google has introduced two new versions of its in-house AI chips, known as tensor processing units (TPUs). The TPU 8tx is designed for running AI workloads on sensitive data, while the TPU 8i Max targets large-scale applications that need more computing power and memory. The launch brings Google’s eighth-generation TPU lineup to four systems. The company unveiled the first two, the TPU 8t and TPU 8i, at its Cloud Next event in April. Google said this is the first time it has introduced four TPU systems in a single year. The TPU 8tx is aimed at governments and regulated businesses that need to run AI models and agents on private data. It uses the same silicon as Google’s TPU 8t training chip and works alongside AMD’s fifth-generation EPYC processors. Its security features are built into the hardware, keeping data and model weights isolated and protected while the chip is running. The system can also handle smaller training jobs, allowing teams to fine-tune models on private data and run other workloads without moving that data between clusters. Google describes the chip as offering “hardware-level confidential compute for private fine-tuning and secure agent execution”. Its faster network design also reduces latency by up to 50% compared with the TPU 8t, the company said. The TPU 8i Max takes a different approach, combining two TPU 8i chips in a single package. Compared with the standard 8i, it doubles computing power, on-chip memory and memory bandwidth. Google is targeting workloads where access to memory can become a bottleneck, including AI agents working across entire codebases and systems analysing hours of video. The design also fits twice as much silicon into the same rack space, increasing the computing capacity data centres can run per square metre and per watt. Google has not disclosed pricing or exact release dates for either chip. Amin Vahdat, the company’s chief technologist for AI and infrastructure, wrote in the blog post that Google’s TPU business now has nearly 100 customers. They include frontier AI labs, trading firms, retailers and universities. TPUs are among the main alternatives to Nvidia’s chips for AI workloads. Google makes them available through its cloud business and works with four design partners to develop them. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Wayve will train its self-driving AI on CoreWeave’s cloud
2026-11-09

Wayve will train its self-driving AI on CoreWeave’s cloud

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
London self-driving startup Wayve will train, test and simulate its AI Driver on CoreWeave’s Nvidia Blackwell cloud under a multi-year deal. Terms were not disclosed. CoreWeave shares fell about 3% before the market opened. Wayve, the London startup building self-driving software, will train its AI models on CoreWeave’s cloud under a multi-year deal, CoreWeave said on Monday. Neither company disclosed the value or length of the agreement. Wayve will use Nvidia Blackwell chips on CoreWeave Cloud to train, test and simulate its AI Driver. The software learns from driving data gathered in more than 70 countries. Wayve started out on Weights & Biases, an AI model-tracking tool that CoreWeave bought last year. “Our AI Driver gets better by running this data flywheel: gathering real-world driving experience, training on it, evaluating it, and deploying only what outperforms what is already on the road,” said Silvius Rus, Wayve’s senior vice president of engineering. Wayve doesn’t build cars. It licenses its software to carmakers and ride-hailing firms. Uber started carrying passengers in Wayve-driven cars in London in September, with a safety driver in every car, and Mercedes-Benz plans to put the AI Driver in its production cars. The startup, valued at $8.6B in February, counts Microsoft and Nvidia among its backers. It has used Microsoft’s Azure cloud to train its models since 2022, so the CoreWeave deal may not be exclusive. For CoreWeave, the deal is a push beyond chatbot makers into “physical AI,” such as cars and robots. Microsoft made up about two-thirds of its revenue last year, Stocktwits reported. This year it has added deals with Meta and Jane Street. The news did little for its shares, which fell about 3% before the market opened and were on course for an 18-month low, Stocktwits said. They had already dropped in a post-midterm sell-off last week. Worries about CoreWeave’s roughly $50B of debt drove a sharp selloff in October. Meta expands AI age checks to move more Instagram teens into Teen Accounts
2026-11-09

Meta expands AI age checks to move more Instagram teens into Teen Accounts

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
Meta is expanding its AI age-detection technology to Instagram users worldwide, aiming to identify more teenage accounts and automatically place them under its Teen Account protections, even when users have registered as adults. The technology looks for signs that an account belongs to someone under 18 and moves it into a Teen Account. These accounts, available across Instagram, Facebook and Messenger, restrict who can contact teenagers, the content they can see and how much time they spend on the apps. Meta said it is still rolling out the technology on Facebook and plans to bring it to Threads worldwide later this year. The company is also introducing new guidance for parents in the US. Starting this month, they will see a video in their Instagram feeds explaining how Meta places teenagers in age-appropriate settings and how they can help confirm their child’s age. In Australia, Meta has begun using Apple’s age-range signal as an additional way to check users’ ages. Parents can ask the App Store to verify their child’s age, after which Apple shares an age range with apps such as Instagram when a teenager downloads them. Meta has long supported legislation requiring app stores to verify users’ ages and obtain parental consent. The company argues that a single verified age signal, shared by the device with all apps, would be more effective and better for privacy than having each platform carry out its own checks. It has also called on TikTok and YouTube to adopt the approach. Meta first introduced its AI system for identifying teenage accounts in the US, UK, Canada and Australia. In May, it expanded the rollout to Brazil and the 27 EU member states. The move comes as the company faces a growing number of lawsuits alleging that its platforms harm young users, Reuters reported. In August, Meta agreed to settlements worth up to $18bn with almost every US state and territory. Under the agreement with 52 attorneys general, the company committed to improving how it identifies teenagers and children under 13. Last month, Meta also settled a case brought by a teenager in a Los Angeles trial examining claims that Instagram and Facebook were designed to be addictive, Reuters reported. Earlier this year, a New Mexico jury found Meta liable for failing to protect children from predators on its platforms and ordered the company to pay $375m in damages. Honda will spend $3B on a new Ohio car plant built around hybrids
2026-11-09

Honda will spend $3B on a new Ohio car plant built around hybrids

The Next Web | 2026-11-09

Visit Original Website
The Honda logo displayed on a smartphone screen. Honda will invest $3B in a new Ohio assembly plant, its first new US vehicle plant since 2008, opening in 2029 with capacity for 240,000 mostly hybrid vehicles a year. It follows Honda’s retreat from EVs and a wider push to build cars in the US to avoid tariffs. Honda will spend $3B on a new car plant in Ohio, its first new US vehicle assembly plant since 2008, the company said on Monday. The plant, between Honda’s existing factories in Marysville and East Liberty, will start production in 2029 and build up to 240,000 vehicles a year. It will focus on hybrids, though it can also make petrol and electric cars. Honda says it will create more than 2,000 jobs and lift its US capacity to about 1.3 million vehicles a year. Bob Nelson, an executive vice president at American Honda, called it “one of the most significant commitments in our history.” Honda is betting on hybrids after pulling back from electric cars. It scrapped its EV lineup for North America in March and indefinitely suspended an $11B EV and battery project in Canada in May, CNBC reported. Its Ohio battery plant, built for those EVs, now makes batteries for data centres. Honda plans 15 new hybrid models worldwide by 2030. It is also expanding hybrid engine and transmission production at plants in Ohio, Alabama and Georgia. Hybrids are selling well in the US, helping Toyota close in on GM as the top-selling carmaker. Carmakers are building more in the US to avoid Trump’s tariffs, which have helped kill a dozen US EV models this year. Audi said last month it would spend more than $4B on its first US plant, in Georgia, and Toyota is moving pickup production to Texas. Honda has warned that doubling US tariffs on Canadian cars to 50% in January would be “catastrophic.” Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks!
